Homotopy groups of shrinking wedges of non-simply connected CW-complexes
Jeremy Brazas

Abstract
In this paper, we study the homotopy groups of a shrinking wedge X X of a sequence { X j } \{X_{j}\} of non-simply connected CW-complexes. Using a combination of generalized covering space theory and shape theory, we construct a canonical homomorphism Θ : π n ​ ( X ) → ∏ j ∈ ℕ ⨁ π 1 ​ ( X ) / π 1 ​ ( X j ) π n ​ ( X j ) , \Theta:\pi_{n}(X)\to\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\pi_{1}(X)/\pi_{1}(X_{j})}\pi_{n}(X_{j}), characterize its image, and prove that Θ \Theta is injective whenever each universal cover X ~ j \widetilde{X}_{j} is ( n − 1 ) (n-1) -connected. These results (1) provide a characterization of the n n -th homotopy group of the shrinking wedge of copies of ℝ ​ ℙ n \mathbb{RP}^{n} , (2) provide a characterization of π 2 \pi_{2} of an arbitrary shrinking wedge, and (3) imply that a shrinking wedge of aspherical CW-complexes is aspherical.



1
Introduction

The shrinking wedge of a sequence
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\dots
of based spaces, which we will denote as
\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j}
, is the usual one-point union
\bigvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j}
but equipped with a topology coarser than the weak topology. In particular, every neighborhood of the wedgepoint
x_{0}
contains
X_{j}
for all but finitely many
j\in\mathbb{N}
. For example,
\mathbb{E}_{m}=\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}S^{m}
is the
m
-dimensional earring space
, which embeds in
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
. While fundamental groups of shrinking wedges of connected CW-complexes are well-understood
[
15
,
6
]
, general methods for characterizing higher homotopy groups remain elusive. It remains an open problem to establish an “infinite Hilton-Milnor Theorem” that would provide a characterization of
\pi_{n}(\mathbb{E}_{m})
,
n>m
.

In 1962, Barratt and Milnor proved that the rational homology groups of
\mathbb{E}_{2}
are non-trivial (and even uncountable) in arbitrarily high dimension
[
3
]
. Compare this with the fact that the reduced homology groups (with any coefficients) of
\bigvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}S^{2}
are only non-trivial in dimension
2
. The apparently “anomalous” behavior of
\mathbb{E}_{2}
is due to the effect of natural, non-trivial, infinitary operations in the higher homotopy groups
\pi_{n}(\mathbb{E}_{2})
,
n>2
and the fact that standard homology groups are only “finitely commutative.”

When local structures in a space allow one to form geometrically represented infinite products in homotopy groups, standard methods in homotopy theory fail to apply. Thus other methods, e.g. shape theory
[
16
]
, generalizations of covering space theory
[
4
,
9
]
, and infinite word theory
[
6
]
are often required. Since infinite products in
\pi_{n}
are formed “at a point,” shrinking wedges present an important case that informs more general scenarios.

In the past two decades some progress has been made toward an understanding of the higher homotopy groups of shrinking wedges. In
[
7
]
, Eda and Kawamura show that
\mathbb{E}_{m}
is
(m-1)
-connected and
\pi_{m}(\mathbb{E}_{m})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}
. In
[
12
]
it is shown that
\pi_{n}(\mathbb{E}_{m})
splits as
\pi_{n+1}((S^{m})^{\mathbb{N}},\mathbb{E}_{m})\oplus\pi_{n}(S^{m})^{\mathbb{N}}
for
n>m
. However, new methods will be need to characterize the elements of
\pi_{n+1}((S^{m})^{\mathbb{N}},\mathbb{E}_{m})
. Some ad-hoc approaches have also appeared, e.g. to show the second homotopy group of the shrinking wedge of tori (see Figure
2
) is trivial
[
8
]
. More recently, the results of Eda-Kawamura were extended in
[
5
]
to other kinds of attachment spaces constructed by attaching a shrinking sequence of spaces to a fixed one-dimensional “core” space, e.g. attaching a shrinking sequence of spheres to an arc, dendrite, Sierpinski carpet, etc. In this paper, we continue the effort to better understand the higher homotopy groups of shrinking wedges. Using a combination of shape theory and generalized covering space theory and the results of
[
5
]
, we establish methods that characterize the effect of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j})
on
\pi_{n}(\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j})
,
n\geq 2
.

To provide more context for the statement of our main result, we briefly recall a standard argument for the usual one-point union. If
X=\bigvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j}
is a wedge of non-simply connected CW-complexes, the universal covering space
\widetilde{X}
, consists of copies of
\widetilde{X}_{j}
(indexed by the coset space
\pi_{1}(X)/\pi_{1}(X_{j})
) that are attached to each other in a tree-like fashion that matches the reduced-word structure of the free product
\pi_{1}(X)=\ast_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{1}(X_{j})
. If
T
is a maximal tree in the 1-skeleton of
\widetilde{X}
, then
\widetilde{X}/T
is homotopy equivalent to
\bigvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigvee_{\pi_{1}(X)/\pi_{1}(X_{j})}\widetilde{X}_{j}
. Since
\pi_{n}(X)\cong\pi_{n}(\widetilde{X})
, we have a surjective homomorphism
\Theta:\pi_{n}(X)\to\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\pi_{1}(X)/\pi_{1}(X_{j})}\pi_{n}(X_{j})
, which can be defined independent of the choice of
T
. Moreover, when each covering space
\widetilde{X}_{j}
is
(n-1)
-connected,
\Theta
is an isomorphism. In the case that some
\widetilde{X}_{j}
are not
(n-1)
connected, other methods for computing homotopy groups of wedges may become relevant; however, the indexing of the wedge summand fully incorporates the effect of
\pi_{1}
on
\pi_{n}
.

There are a few places where standard methods break down for a shrinking wedge
X=\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j}
of connected, non-simply connected CW-complexes. First and foremost,
X
does not have a universal covering space. However, it does have a generalized universal covering space
\widetilde{X}
(and map
p:\widetilde{X}\to X
) in the sense of Fischer and Zastrow
[
9
]
. The structure of
\widetilde{X}
is an “infinite version” of the classical situation. In particular,
\widetilde{X}
also consists of copies of the universal covering spaces
\widetilde{X}_{j}
arranged in a tree-like fashion (in the sense that simple closed curves only exist in individual copies of
\widetilde{X}_{j}
). However, these arrangements will now mimic the reduced infinite-word description of
\pi_{1}(X)

[
6
]
. For example, an infinite product
\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\cdots\in\pi_{1}(X)
where
\ell_{j}\in\pi_{1}(X_{j})
will lift to a path in
\widetilde{X}
that proceeds (in order) through copies of
\widetilde{X}_{1},\widetilde{X}_{2},\widetilde{X}_{3},...
in
\widetilde{X}
. Thus, when
j\to\infty
, one should consider copies of
\widetilde{X}_{j}
in
\widetilde{X}
as being shrinking in size. Since infinite words in
\pi_{1}(X)
may be indexed by countable, dense linear orders, there will be corresponding dense arrangements of the spaces
\widetilde{X}_{j}
within
\widetilde{X}
too. With this description of
\widetilde{X}
, it is possible to choose a uniquely arcwise connected subspace
T\subseteq\widetilde{X}
that is analogous to a maximal tree. However, the collapsing map
\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{X}/T
will rarely be a homotopy equivalence. Moreover, care is required if one wishes to choose
T
to be coherent with a choice of trees in the universal covers over the approximating finite wedge
\bigvee_{j=1}^{k}X_{k}
. Finally, while
X
is “wild” at only a single point,
\widetilde{X}
will be wild at uncountably many points, namely those in the wedgepoint fiber
p^{-1}(x_{0})
.

To overcome the many obstacles laid out in the previous paragraph, we first attach an arc to each space
X_{j}
to form
Y_{j}
and take the endpoint of the added “whisker” to be the basepoint of
Y_{j}
. The universal cover
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
consists of
\widetilde{X}_{j}
with arcs attached to each point in the basepoint fiber of the covering map
\widetilde{X}_{j}\to X_{j}
. Now the generalized universal covering
\widetilde{Y}
is comprised of copies of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
arranged in the same way copies of
\widetilde{X}_{j}
are arranged in
\widetilde{X}
. However, the added arcs will provide “extra space” around which we can perform desired deformations. Most of the technical work in this paper goes toward understanding both the direct construction of
\widetilde{Y}
(Definition
2.8
) and its relationship to the inverse limit of ordinary universal covering spaces. A key insight is that we must coherently choose a maximal tree in each copy of
\widetilde{X}_{j}
appearing within
\widetilde{Y}
. We also use this relationship to prove that the map collapsing each of these (uncountably many) trees to a point is a homotopy equivalence. The resulting quotient
Z
consists of a uniquely arcwise-connected space with copies of a homotopy equivalent quotient of
\widetilde{X}_{j}
attached along points. This puts us precisely in a situation to apply the main result of
[
5
]
. The main result of the current paper is the following theorem.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
n\geq 2
and
X=\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j}
be a shrinking wedge of connected CW-complexes
X_{j}
. Then there is a canonical homomorphism

\Theta:\pi_{n}(X)\to\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\pi_{1}(X)/\pi_{1}(X_{j})}\pi_{n}(X_{j}),

which is injective if each
X_{j}
has an
(n-1)
-connected universal covering space.

The injectivity of
\Theta
in Theorem
1.1
is the isomorphism from
[
7
]
if each
X_{j}
is simply connected and thus
(n-1)
-connected. In the arbitrary case, we are still able to characterize the image of
\Theta
in terms of a natural topology on
\pi_{1}(X)
(see Remark
6.6
, which follows from Theorem
6.3
). We remark on some immediate applications and cases of interest.

Example 1.2
.

Consider the shrinking wedge
X=\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{RP}^{n}
of copies of real projective
n
-space. The universal cover
S^{n}
of
\mathbb{RP}^{n}
is
(n-1)
-connected and so
\pi_{n}\left(X\right)
embeds as a subgroup of

\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\pi_{1}(X)/\pi_{1}(\mathbb{RP}^{n})}\pi_{n}(\mathbb{RP}^{n})\cong\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\pi_{1}(X)/\pi_{1}(\mathbb{RP}^{n})}\mathbb{Z}\cong\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{c}}\mathbb{Z}.

It is possible construct the generalized universal covering space
\widetilde{X}
similar to how one might describe the universal cover of
\bigvee_{j=1}^{k}\mathbb{RP}^{n}
as a tree-like arrangement of
n
-spheres. Explicitly, we could start with the generalized universal covering space
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{1}
of the
1
-dimensional earring space
\mathbb{E}_{1}
, which is a topological
\mathbb{R}
-tree and acts as a generalized Caley graph
[
10
]
. Every lift of a loop parameterizing the
j
-th circle of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
parameterizes an “edge” in
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{1}
. Replacing each of these edges with a copy of
S^{n}
(replacing endpoints with a choice of antipodal points) and topologizing in a suitable fashion yields
\widetilde{X}
(see Figure
1
). This is an instructive case to consider when reading the remainder of the paper as we understand
\pi_{n}(\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{RP}^{n})
by using inverse limits to characterize and deform the structure of
\widetilde{X}
.

Figure 1:
The generalized universal cover
\widetilde{X}
of
\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{RP}^{2}
seems impossible to visualize as a whole but it will contain homeomorphic copies of the space illustrated here, namely, an arc where the closure of each component of the complement of the ternary Cantor set in that arc has been replaced by a
2
-sphere and such that the diameters of the spheres approach
0
.
\widetilde{X}
will also contain arrangements of
2
-spheres indexed by every other countable linear order type. Each point in the Cantor set shown here, will be a “branch point” of uncountable valence; every possible linear arrangement of spheres being attached at every branch point multiple times.

Theorem
1.1
also provides a characterization of
\pi_{2}
for an arbitrary shrinking wedge since the universal covering spaces
\widetilde{X}_{j}
are always
1
-connected.

Corollary 1.3
.

If
X=\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j}
is a shrinking wedge of connected CW-complexes, then there is a canonical injective homomorphism

\Theta:\pi_{2}(X)\to\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\pi_{1}(X)/\pi_{1}(X_{j})}\pi_{2}(X_{j}).

There are many algebraic statements, which are immediate consequences of embedding statements like Theorem
1.1
and Corollary
1.3
, e.g.
\pi_{2}(\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j})
is torsion-free if and only if
\pi_{2}(X_{j})
is torsion-free for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Recall that a path-connected space
Y
is
aspherical
if
\pi_{n}(Y)=0
for all
n\geq 2
. Theorem
1.1
also implies the first part of the following theorem; the second part must be proved separately (see Section
6.4
).

Theorem 1.4
.

If
X_{j}
is an aspherical CW-complex for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j}
is aspherical. Moreover, if each
X_{j}
is locally finite, then the generalized universal covering space
\widetilde{X}
is contractible.

Example 1.5
.

Corollary
1.3
implies that the shrinking wedge of tori
\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{T}
(see Figure
2
) is aspherical. Previously, it was only known that
\pi_{2}(\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{T})=0

[
8
]
. Similarly, a shrinking wedge of any sequence of orientable surfaces with positive (or infinite) genus is aspherical.

Figure 2:
The shrinking wedge of tori is aspherical and has a contractible generalized universal covering space.

2
Preliminaries and Notation

All topological spaces in this paper are assumed to be Hausdorff. Throughout,
I
denotes the unit interval
[0,1]
and a
path
is a map
\alpha:I\to X
. We write
\alpha\cdot\beta
for the concatenation of paths when
\alpha(1)=\beta(0)
and
\alpha^{-}
for the reverse path
\alpha^{-}(t)=\alpha(1-t)
. If
[a,b]\subseteq I
and
\alpha:I\to X
is a path, we may simply write
[\alpha|_{[a,b]}]
to denote the path-homotopy class
[\alpha|_{[a,b]}\circ h]
where
h:[0,1]\to[a,b]
is the unique increasing linear homeomorphism.

We will generally represent elements of the
n
-th homotopy group
\pi_{n}(X,x)
,
n\geq 1
by relative maps
(I^{n},\partial I^{n})\to(X,x)
. When the basepoint
x
is clear from context, we will suppress it from our notation and simply write
\pi_{n}(X)
.

We say that a homotopy
H:X\times I\to Y
is
constant on

A\subseteq X
(or is
relative to
A
) if for all
\mathbf{x}\in A
,
H(\mathbf{x},t)
is constant as
t
varies. If
H
is constant on the basepoint
x_{0}
, then we call
H
a based homotopy. A
based homotopy equivalence
is based map
f:(X,x)\to(Y,y)
where there is a based homotopy inverse
g:(Y,y)\to(X,x)
and based homotopies
id_{X}\simeq g\circ f
and
id_{Y}\simeq f\circ g
.

A
Peano continuum
is a connected locally path-connected compact metric space. The Hahn-Mazurkiewicz Theorem
[
17
, Theorem 8.14]
implies that a Hausdorff space is a Peano continuum if and only if there exists a continuous surjection
I\to X
. A Peano continuum which is uniquely arcwise connected is a
dendrite
.

2.1
Shrinking wedges and their fundamental groups

Given a collection
(X_{j},x_{j})
,
j\in S
of spaces, let
\bigvee_{j\in S}(X_{j},x_{j})
(or
\bigvee_{j\in S}X_{j}
when basepoints are clear from context) denote the usual one point union with the weak topology. We will refer to the natural basepoint
x_{0}
as the
wedgepoint
.

Definition 2.1
.

The
shrinking wedge
of an infinite sequence
(X_{j},x_{j})
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
of based spaces is the space
\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}(X_{j},x_{j})
with the underlying set of
\bigvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j}
but with the following topology:
U\subseteq X
is open if and only if
U\cap X_{j}
is open in
X_{j}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and if
x_{0}\in U
implies
X_{j}\subseteq U
for all but finitely many
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

For both standard and shrinking wedges, we will refer to each space
X_{j}
as a
wedge summand
.

In the remainder of this section, we will assume that, for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, the space
X_{j}
is a connected CW-complex basepoint
x_{j}
that serves as the basepoint of
X_{j}
. Let
X=\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j}
be the shrinking wedge and for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
X_{\leq k}=\bigvee_{j=1}^{k}X_{j}
be finite wedge of the first
k
spaces. Define

•

R_{k+1,k}:X_{\leq k+1}\to X_{\leq k}
to be the retraction that collapses
X_{k+1}
to
x_{0}
,

•

R_{k}:X\to X_{\leq k}
to be the retraction that collapses
\bigcup_{j>k}X_{j}
to
x_{0}
.

The canonical induced map
X\to\varprojlim_{k}X_{\leq k}
,
x\mapsto(R_{k}(x))
is a homeomorphism; we will sometimes identify
X
with this inverse limit representation.

We identify
\pi_{1}(X_{\leq k})
with the free product
\ast_{j=1}^{k}\pi_{1}(X_{j})
. If
\pi_{1}(X_{j})=1
for all but finitely many
j
, then we arrive at the finitely generated case
\pi_{1}(X)\cong\pi_{1}(X_{\leq k})
for some
k
. To avoid this situation we will assume that
\pi_{1}(X_{j})\neq 1
for infinitely many
j
. By grouping and rearranging some of the
\pi_{1}(X_{j})
, we may assume that
\pi_{1}(X_{j})\neq 1
for all
j
. In this case,
\pi_{1}(X)
will be uncountable and not isomorphic to the infinite free product of the groups
\pi_{1}(X_{j})
. We recall the two main approaches to characterizing the elements of
\pi_{1}(X)
: (1) the inverse limit/shape theoretic approach and (2) infinite reduced words.

The idea of the shape theoretic approach is to embed
\pi_{1}(X)
into an inverse limit of the fundamental groups of the approximating projections. It is well-known that shrinking wedges of CW-complexes are
\pi_{1}
-shape injective in the following sense.

Theorem 2.2
.

[
15
]

If
X=\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j}
is a shrinking wedge of CW-complexes, then the canonical homeomorphism
\phi_{X}:\pi_{1}(X)\to\varprojlim_{k}\pi_{1}(X_{\leq k})
,
\phi_{X}(\alpha)=((R_{k})_{\#}(\alpha))
is injective.

Thus
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(X)
is non-trivial if and only if there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(R_{k})_{\#}(\alpha)\neq 1
in the free product
\ast_{j=1}^{k}\pi_{1}(X_{j})
.

The second approach assigns a unique infinite word to each element of
\pi_{1}(X)
. A
word
is a function
w
from a countable linearly ordered set
\overline{w}
to
\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{1}(X_{j})
(assuming
\pi_{1}(X_{j})\cap\pi_{1}(X_{j^{\prime}})=\{1\}
when
j\neq j^{\prime}
) such that
w^{-1}(\pi_{1}(X_{j}))
is finite for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. If
v
is another word and there is an order isomorphism
\kappa:\overline{w}\to\overline{v}
such that
v\circ\kappa=w
, then we consider
w
and
v
isomorphic (and write
w\cong v
). The collection of all isomorphism classes words
\mathscr{W}
is a set.

Given a word
w
and finite set
F\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, we define the projection word
w_{F}:\overline{w_{F}}\to\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{1}(X_{j})
to be the finite word obtained by deleting all letters in
\pi_{1}(X_{j})
,
j\notin F
. More precisely,
\overline{w_{F}}=\{\ell\in\overline{w}\mid w(\ell)\in\bigcup_{j\in F}\pi_{1}(X_{j})\}
and
\overline{w_{F}}(\ell)=\overline{w}(\ell)
whenever
\ell\in\overline{w_{F}}
. We may regard
w_{F}
as an unreduced word representing an element of the free product
\ast_{j\in F}\pi_{1}(X_{j})
.

Given
w,v\in\mathscr{W}
, we write
w\sim v
if for every finite subset
F\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, the reduced representatives of
w_{F}
and
v_{F}
in
\ast_{j\in F}\pi_{1}(X_{j})
are equal. Since
\sim
is an equivalence relation on
\mathscr{W}
, we let
[w]
denote the equivalence class of
w
. The set
\varoast_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{1}(X_{j})=\mathscr{W}/\mathord{\sim}
becomes a group with the operation
[w][v]=[wv]
where
wv
is the concatenation of the reduced words with
\overline{wv}
defined as the linear order sum
\overline{w}+\overline{v}
. The identity
e
or “empty word” is the equivalence class of the identity on the 1-point ordered set
\{1\}\to\{1\}
.

A word
w\in\mathscr{W}
is
reduced
if (1) whenever
w=avb
, we have
[v]\neq e
and (2) whenever
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
are consecutive elements in
\overline{w}
,
w(\ell)
and
w(\ell^{\prime})
lie in distinct groups
\pi_{1}(X_{j})
. Intuitively,
w
is reduced if it has no trivial subwords (including
w
itself) and if it is not possible to combine any existing consecutive letters. It is known that for every word
w\in\mathscr{W}
, there exists a reduced word
v
, unique up to isomorphism, such that
[w]=[v]
(see
[
6
, Theorem 1.4]
).

The projection maps
\varoast_{j}\pi_{1}(X_{j})\to\pi_{1}(X_{\leq k})
,
[w]\mapsto[w_{F}]
where
F=\{1,2,\dots,k\}
agree with the bonding maps
(R_{k+1,k})_{\#}:\pi_{1}(X_{\leq k+1})\to\pi_{1}(X_{\leq k})
and induce a homomorphism
\psi:\varoast_{j}\pi_{1}(X_{j})\to\varprojlim_{k}\pi_{1}(X_{\leq k})
such that
\operatorname{Im}(\psi)=\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{X})
.

Given a non-constant loop
\beta:I\to X
based at
x_{0}
, let
\overline{\beta}
be the set of connected components of
\beta^{-1}(X\backslash\{x_{0}\})
with the linear ordering inherited from
I
. There is a well-defined word
w_{\beta}:\overline{\beta}\to\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{1}(X_{j})
given by
w_{\beta}((a,b))=[\alpha|_{[a,b]}]
. Now
\chi([\beta])=[w_{\beta}]
defines a group isomorphism satisfying
\psi\circ\chi=\phi_{X}
.

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{X}}
\textstyle{\varprojlim_{k}\pi_{1}(X_{\leq k})}
\textstyle{\varoast_{j}\pi_{1}(X_{j})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}

Definition 2.3
.

We say that a loop
\beta:I\to X
based at
x_{0}
is
reduced
if
\beta
is constant or if
w_{\beta}
is a reduced word in
\mathscr{W}
.

Considering the above diagram, it follows that every loop
\alpha:I\to X
based at
x_{0}
is path-homotopic to a reduced loop
\beta
. Moreover, reduced loop representatives of homotopy classes are unique in the following sense: if
\beta
and
\gamma
are path-homotopic reduced loops, then there is an order-isomorphism
\kappa:\overline{\beta}\to\overline{\gamma}
, such that if
(a,b)\in\overline{\beta}
and
\kappa((a,b))=(c,d)\in\overline{\gamma}
, then
\beta|_{[a,b]}\simeq\gamma|_{[c,d]}
as loops in one of the spaces
X_{j}
. Therefore, if
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(X)
, we may also use the symbol
\alpha
to denote a choice of reduced loop in
\alpha
.

2.2
The locally path connected coreflection

Because inverse limits of locally path connected spaces are not always locally path connected, we require the following construction.

Definition 2.4
.

The
locally path-connected coreflection
of a space
X
is the space
\operatorname{lpc}(X)
with the same underlying set as
X
but with topology generated by the basis consisting of all path components of the open sets in
X
.

The topology of
\operatorname{lpc}(X)
is finer than that of
X
thus the identity function
id:\operatorname{lpc}(X)\to X
is continuous. It is well-known that
\operatorname{lpc}(X)
is locally path connected and that
\operatorname{lpc}(X)=X
if and only if
X
is already locally path connected. The construction of
\operatorname{lpc}(X)
defines a functor
\operatorname{lpc}:\mathbf{Top}\to\mathbf{Lpc}
from the category of topological spaces to the full subcategory of locally path connected spaces. This functor is a coreflection in the sense that
\operatorname{lpc}
is right adjoint to the inclusion functor
\mathbf{Lpc}\to\mathbf{Top}
. In other words, if
Z
is locally path connected, then a function
f:Z\to X
is continuous if and only if
f:Z\to\operatorname{lpc}(X)
is continuous. In particular,
X
and
\operatorname{lpc}(X)
share the same set of continuous functions from
I^{n}
. It follows that
id:\operatorname{lpc}(X)\to X
is a bijective weak homotopy equivalence.

Since the direct product of locally path-connected spaces is locally path connected,
\operatorname{lpc}(\prod_{j}X_{j})\cong\prod_{j}\operatorname{lpc}(X_{j})
in
\mathbf{Top}
. In particular,
\operatorname{lpc}(X\times I)=\operatorname{lpc}(X)\times I
allows one to prove the following proposition.

Proposition 2.5
.

If
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to X
are (based or unbased) homotopy inverses, then so are
\operatorname{lpc}(f):\operatorname{lpc}(X)\to\operatorname{lpc}(Y)
and
\operatorname{lpc}(g):\operatorname{lpc}(Y)\to\operatorname{lpc}(X)
.

One should be wary of limits of inverse systems in
\mathbf{Lpc}
because inverse limits of locally path connected spaces in
\mathbf{Lpc}
and
\mathbf{Top}
do not always agree. If
\varprojlim_{j}X_{j}
is an inverse limit in
\mathbf{Top}
of locally path connected spaces (viewed as a subspace of
\prod_{j}X_{j}
), then
\operatorname{lpc}(\varprojlim_{j}X_{j})
is the space that gives the limit of the same inverse system in
\mathbf{Lpc}
.

2.3
Generalized universal covering maps

When each
X_{j}
is a connected, non-simply connected CW-complex,
\bigcurlyvee_{j}X_{j}
will not have a universal covering space. However
\bigcurlyvee_{j}X_{j}
always admits a generalized universal covering space in the sense of Fischer-Zastrow
[
9
]
. The idea behind this notion of “generalized (universal) covering map” is to use the lifting properties of covering maps as the definition and work internal to the category of path-connected, locally path-connected spaces.

Definition 2.6
.

A map
q:E\to X
is a
generalized covering map
if
E
is non-empty, path connected, and locally path connected and if for any map
f:(Y,y)\to(X,x)
from a path-connected, locally path-connected space
Y
and point
e\in q^{-1}(x)
such that
f_{\#}(\pi_{1}(Y,y))\leq q_{\#}(\pi_{1}(E,e))
, there is a unique map
\widetilde{f}:(Y,y)\to(E,e)
such that
q\circ\widetilde{f}=f
. Moreover, if
E
is simply connected, we call
q
a
generalized universal covering map
and
E
a
generalized universal covering space
.

Unlike ordinary covering maps, based generalized covering maps are closed under composition and form a complete category
[
4
]
. The following proposition follows immediately from the definition and standard covering space theory arguments.

Proposition 2.7
.

If
q:(E,e_{0})\to(X,x_{0})
satisfies all properties of being a generalized covering map except for the assumption that
E
is locally path connected, then the induced homomorphism
q_{\#}:\pi_{n}(E,e_{0})\to\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
is an injection for
n=1
and an isomorphism for all
n\geq 2
.

Every (universal) covering map (in the usual sense)
p:E\to X
where
E
is path connected and
X
is locally path connected is a generalized (universal) covering map. If
p:E\to X
is a generalized universal covering map, then
p
is an ordinary covering map if and only if
X
is semilocally simply connected. We recall the following standard construction from covering space theory
[
18
]
.

Definition 2.8
.

Whenever
X
is a space with given basepoint
x_{0}\in X
, let
\widetilde{X}
be the space of path-homotopy classes
[\alpha]
of paths
\alpha:(I,0)\to(X,x_{0})
. An open neighborhood of
[\alpha]
is a set of the form
N([\alpha],U)=\{[\alpha\cdot\epsilon]\mid\epsilon(I)\subseteq U\}
where
U
is an open neighborhood of
\alpha(1)
in
X
. This topology is the so-called
whisker topology
on
\widetilde{X}
. The homotopy class of the constant path at
x_{0}
, which we denote as
\widetilde{x}_{0}
, is the basepoint of
\widetilde{X}
.

The endpoint projection map
p:\widetilde{X}\to X
,
p([\alpha])=\alpha(1)
is a continuous surjection, which is open if and only if
X
is locally path connected and provides a candidate for a generalized universal covering map.

Remark 2.9

(Standard Lifts of Paths)
.

Every path in
X
lifts uniquely to
\widetilde{X}
relative to a chosen starting point. Suppose
[\beta]\in\widetilde{X}
and
\alpha:(I,0)\to(X,\beta(1))
is a path. Define paths
\alpha_{s}:I\to X
,
s\in I
by
\alpha_{s}(t)=\alpha(st)
. The function
\widetilde{\alpha}:(I,0)\to(\widetilde{X},[\beta])
,
\widetilde{\alpha}(s)=[\beta\cdot\alpha_{s}]
defines a continuous lift of
\alpha
starting at
[\beta]
(c.f.
[
9
, Lemma 2.4]
), which we refer to as a
standard lift
of
\alpha
.

Remark
2.9
ensures that
p:\widetilde{X}\to X
always has path-lifting. According to
[
9
, Prop. 2.14]
,
p:\widetilde{X}\to X
is a generalized universal covering map if and only if
p
has the unique path-lifting property, that is, if the lift described in Remark
2.9
is the
only
lift of
\alpha
starting at
[\beta]
. In general, this does not have to happen
[
9
, Example 2.7]
. However, many sufficient conditions are known.

Theorem 2.10
.

[
9
]

If
X
is metrizable and path-connected,
x_{0}\in X
, and the canonical homomorphism
\phi:\pi_{1}(X,x_{0})\to\check{\pi}_{1}(X,x_{0})
to the first shape homotopy group is injective, then
p:\widetilde{X}\to X
is a generalized universal covering map.

In the case of a shrinking wedge
X=\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j}
of CW-complexes
X_{j}
, the homomorphism
\phi:\pi_{1}(X,x_{0})\to\check{\pi}_{1}(X,x_{0})
is precisely that from Theorem
2.2
.

Corollary 2.11
.

Every shrinking wedge of CW-complexes admits a generalized universal covering space.

The next theorem guarantees ensures that whenever a generalized universal covering map exists, it may be constructed as in Definition
2.8
.

Theorem 2.12
.

[
4
, Section 5]

If there exists a generalized universal covering map
q:(E,e_{0})\to(X,x_{0})
, then there exists a homeomorphism
h:(\widetilde{X},\tilde{x}_{0})\to(E,e_{0})
such that
q\circ h=p
.

We will also have need of the following separation axiom.

Lemma 2.13
.

[
9
, Lemmas 2.10 and 2.11]

If
p:\widetilde{X}\to X
is a generalized universal covering map where
X
is Hausdorff, then
\widetilde{X}
is Hausdorff.

Definition 2.14

(A topology on the fundamental group)
.

When
p:\widetilde{X}\to X
is a generalized universal covering map with respect to a basepoint
x_{0}\in X
, the fiber
p^{-1}(x_{0})
is precisely the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X,x_{0})
. In particular,
\pi_{1}(X,x_{0})
naturally inherits a topology as a subspace of
\widetilde{X}
, which we also refer to as the
whisker topology
. Since
\widetilde{X}
is Hausdorff by the previous lemma,
\pi_{1}(X,x_{0})
is Hausdorff with this topology.

If one has a map
q:E\to X
, which has all of the properties of a generalized universal covering map except for
E
being locally path connected, the locally path connected coreflection provides a “quick fix.” Indeed, for any path connected space
X
, the identity function
id:\operatorname{lpc}(X)\to X
is a generalized covering map.

Proposition 2.15
.

If
q:E\to X
has all of the properties of a generalized universal covering map except for the assumption that
E
is locally path connected, then
q:\operatorname{lpc}(E)\to X
is a generalized universal covering map.

Based generalized covering maps are closed under pullback using
\operatorname{lpc}
: If
q:(\widetilde{X},\tilde{x}_{0})\to(X,x)
is a based generalized covering map and
f:(Y,y_{0})\to(X,x_{0})
is a based map, then there is a pullback generalized covering map (of
p
over
f
)
p:(\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{y}_{0})\to(Y,y_{0})
and a map
\widetilde{f}:(\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{y}_{0})\to(\widetilde{X},\tilde{x}_{0})
such that
q\circ\widetilde{f}=f\circ p
. In particular, we let
C
be the path component of
(\tilde{x}_{0},y_{0})
in the ordinary topological pullback
\widetilde{X}\times_{X}Y=\{([\alpha],y)\in\widetilde{X}\times Y\mid f(y)=\alpha(1)\}
, set
\widetilde{Y}=\operatorname{lpc}(C)
and let
p
and
\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{Y}\to\widetilde{X}
be the restrictions of the projection maps.

Proposition 2.16
.

If
q:(\widetilde{X},\tilde{x}_{0})\to(X,x)
is a based generalized universal covering map and
f:(Y,y_{0})\to(X,x_{0})
induces an injection on fundamental groups, then the pullback
p:\widetilde{Y}\to Y
of
q
over
f
is also a generalized universal covering.

The next corollary follows from straightforward lifting arguments so we omit the proof.

Corollary 2.17
.

If
f:(Y,y_{0})\to(X,x_{0})
is a based homotopy equivalence and
q:(\widetilde{X},\tilde{x}_{0})\to(X,x_{0})
is a generalized universal covering map, then there exists a generalized universal covering map
p:(\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{y}_{0})\to(Y,y_{0})
and a based homotopy equivalence
\widetilde{f}:(\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{y}_{0})\to(\widetilde{X},\tilde{x}_{0})
such that
q\circ\widetilde{f}=f\circ p
.

Because Theorem
2.2
holds for shrinking wedges, there is another way to construct their generalized universal covering maps, which we detail in the next remark.

Remark 2.18

(Inverse Limits of Coverings)
.

Let
(X,x_{0})=\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}(X_{j},x_{j})
be a shrinking wedge of connected CW-complexes with inverse limit presentation
\varprojlim_{k}(X_{\leq k},R_{k+1,k})
. If
q_{\leq k}:(\widetilde{X}_{\leq k},\tilde{x}_{0})\to(X_{\leq k},x_{0})
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
are the universal covering maps, we have the following situation.

\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{(\widetilde{X}_{\leq 3},\tilde{x}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{\leq 3}}
\textstyle{(\widetilde{X}_{\leq 2},\widetilde{x}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{\leq 2}}
\textstyle{(\widetilde{X}_{\leq 1},\widetilde{x}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{\leq 1}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(X_{\leq 3},x_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{3,2}}
\textstyle{(X_{\leq 2},x_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{2,1}}
\textstyle{(X_{\leq 1},x_{0})}

Since
\widetilde{X}_{\leq k}
is locally path connected and simply connected, the lifting property of the maps
q_{\leq k}
ensures that we have maps
\widetilde{R}_{k+1,k}:(\widetilde{X}_{\leq k+1},\tilde{x}_{0})\to(\widetilde{X}_{\leq k},\tilde{x}_{0})
making the diagram below commute. The result is an inverse sequence of based generalized universal covering maps. Let
\widehat{X}=\varprojlim_{k}(\widetilde{X}_{\leq k},\widetilde{R}_{k+1,k})
and
\widehat{q}=\varprojlim_{k}q_{\leq k}
denote the respective inverse limits. Let
\widetilde{R}_{k}:\widehat{X}\to\widetilde{X}_{\leq k}
be the projection map for

.

\scriptstyle{q_{\leq 3}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{R}_{3,2}}
\textstyle{(\widetilde{X}_{\leq 2},\widetilde{x}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{\leq 2}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{R}_{2,1}}
\textstyle{(\widetilde{X}_{\leq 1},\widetilde{x}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{\leq 1}}
\textstyle{X}

\scriptstyle{R_{2,1}}
\textstyle{(X_{\leq 1},x_{0})}

Since
\widehat{X}
need not be path-connected, we let
\widehat{X}_{0}
be the path component of
\widehat{x}_{0}=(\tilde{x}_{0},\tilde{x}_{0},\tilde{x}_{0},\dots)\in\widehat{X}
. Taking
\widehat{q}_{0}:(\widehat{X}_{0},\widehat{x}_{0})\to(X,x_{0})
to be the restriction of
\widehat{q}
, a direct argument shows that
\widehat{q}_{0}
has all the properties of a generalized covering map except that
\widehat{X}_{0}
need not be locally path connected. In particular,
\widehat{q}_{0\#}:\pi_{1}(\widehat{X}_{0},\widehat{x}_{0})\to\pi_{1}(X,x_{0})
is injective. By Proposition
2.15
and Theorem
2.12
,
\widehat{q}_{0}:\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{X}_{0})\to X
is a generalized covering map.

Moreover,
\widehat{X}_{0}
is simply connected: Given a loop
\widetilde{\alpha}:(I,\{0,1\})\to(\widehat{X}_{0},\widehat{x}_{0})
,
\widetilde{R}_{k}\circ\widetilde{\alpha}
is null-homotopic since
\widetilde{X}_{\leq k}
is simply connected. Therefore,
R_{k}\circ(\widehat{q}\circ\widetilde{\alpha})=q_{\leq k}\circ\widetilde{R}_{k}\circ\widetilde{\alpha}
is null-homotopic for all
k
. Since
\phi_{X}:\pi_{1}(X,x_{0})\to\varprojlim_{k}\pi_{1}(X_{\leq k},x_{0})
is injective (Theorem
2.2
) and
\phi_{X}([\widehat{q}\circ\widetilde{\alpha}])=1
, we have
[\widehat{q}_{0}\circ\widetilde{\alpha}]=1
. Since
\widehat{q}_{0}
is
\pi_{1}
-injective (Proposition
2.7
),
\widetilde{\alpha}
is null-homotopic.

Since
\widehat{X}_{0}
is simply connected and
id:\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{X}_{0})\to\widehat{X}_{0}
is a weak homotopy equivalence,
\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{X}_{0})
is simply connected. According to Proposition
2.15
,
\widehat{q}_{0}:\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{X}_{0})\to X
satisfies all criteria to be a generalized
universal
covering map. Theorem
2.12
now implies that there is a unique homeomorphism
\phi_{X}:(\widetilde{X},\tilde{x}_{0})\to(\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{X}_{0}),\widehat{x}_{0})
such that
\widehat{q}_{0}\circ\phi=q
. We use “
\phi_{X}
” to denote this map because its restriction to the basepoint-fiber:
p^{-1}(x_{0})\to\varprojlim_{k}q_{k}^{-1}(x_{0})
is precisely the homomorphism
\phi_{X}
from Theorem
2.2
.

\textstyle{\widetilde{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{X}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\widehat{X}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{q}_{0}}
\textstyle{X}

Corollary 2.19
.

Let
X=\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j}
be a shrinking wedge and
\phi_{X}:\widetilde{X}\to\widehat{X}_{0}
and
\widetilde{R}_{k}:\widehat{X}\to\widetilde{X}_{\leq k}
be defined as in Remark
2.18
. If
W
is locally path connected and
f:W\to\widetilde{X}
is a function, then the following are equivalent:

(1)

f:W\to\widetilde{X}
is continuous,

(2)

\phi_{X}\circ f:W\to\widehat{X}_{0}
is continuous,

(3)

\widetilde{R}_{k}\circ\phi_{X}\circ f:W\to\widetilde{X}_{\leq k}
is continuous for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

3
Attaching whiskers and choosing maximal trees

At this point, we being to fix spaces and establish notation that will be used throughout the remainder of the paper. We assume that, for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, the space
X_{j}
is a fixed connected, non-simply connected, CW-complex with a single
0
-cell
x_{j}
that serves as the basepoint of
X_{j}
. Let
q_{j}:\widetilde{X}_{j}\to X_{j}
be the universal cover of the individual wedge summands. We use the notation consistent with that in Section
2.1
, namely,
X=\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j}
is the shrinking wedge with wedgepoint
x_{0}
and for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
X_{\leq k}=\bigvee_{j=1}^{k}X_{j}\subseteq X
. Additionally,
R_{k+1,k}:X_{\leq k+1}\to X_{\leq k}
and
R_{k}:X\to X_{\leq k}
are the canonical retractions and
q_{\leq k}:\widetilde{X}_{\leq k}\to X_{\leq k}
is the universal covering map of the finite wedge. We apply the construction in Remark
2.18
to the retractions
R_{k+1,k}
and covering maps
q_{\leq k}
and fix the notation used there. Since the canonical homomorphism
\phi_{X}:\pi_{1}(X)\to\varprojlim_{k}\pi_{1}(X_{\leq k})
is injective (Theorem
2.2
)
X
admits a generalized universal covering map
q:\widetilde{X}\to X
and there is a homeomorphism
\phi_{X}:\widetilde{X}\to\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{X}_{0})
such that
\widehat{q}_{0}\circ\phi_{X}=q
.

3.1
Attaching whiskers: replacing
X
with
Y

Let
Y_{j}=X_{j}\times\{0\}\cup\{x_{j}\}\times I
be the subspace of
X_{j}\times I
with basepoint
y_{j}=(x_{j},1)
. By identifying
X_{j}
with
X_{j}\times\{0\}
, we may treat
Y_{j}
as the CW-complex consisting of
X_{j}
and a “whisker”
\mathbf{e}_{j}=\{x_{j}\}\times I
attached at
x_{j}
. Let
p_{j}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\to Y_{j}
be the universal covering map. The homotopy extension property of the pair
(X_{j},x_{j})
allows us to choose a retraction
\mu_{j}:X_{j}\times I\to Y_{j}
so that
\mu_{j,1}(x)=\mu_{j}(x,1)
is a based homotopy inverse of the quotient map
\zeta_{j}:Y_{j}\to X_{j}
that collapses the whisker
\{x_{j}\}\times I
to
x_{j}
(see Figure
3
). In particular,
\zeta_{j}\circ\mu_{j}
is a based homotopy from
id_{X_{j}}
to
\zeta_{j}\circ\mu_{j,1}
. A based homotopy from
id_{Y_{j}}
to
\mu_{j,1}\circ\zeta_{j}
is illustrated in Figure
4
.

Figure 3:
The based homotopy equivalence
\zeta_{j}:Y_{j}\to X_{j}
, which collapses the arc
\mathbf{e}_{j}
.

Figure 4:
The based homotopy
Y_{j}\times I\to Y_{j}
from
id_{Y_{j}}
to
\mu_{j,1}\circ\zeta_{j}
is a composition which first applies
\mu_{j}
to the subspace
X_{j}\times I
, which is illustrated as a cylinder. The square
\mathbf{e}_{j}\times I
is mapped to
\mathbf{e}_{j}
so that the upper left triangle maps to
y_{j}
and the lower right triangle is projected linearly.

Let
Y=\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}(Y_{j},y_{j})
be the shrinking wedge with wedgepoint
y_{0}
and for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
Y_{\leq k}=\bigvee_{j=1}^{k}Y_{j}
be the finite wedge viewed as retractions of
Y
. The respective canonical retractions will be denoted by
r_{k+1,k}:Y_{\leq k+1}\to Y_{\leq k}
and
r_{k}:Y\to Y_{\leq k}
and the universal covering maps by
p_{j}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\to Y_{j}
and
p_{\leq k}:\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\to Y_{\leq k}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

The quotient map
\zeta:Y\to X
that collapses
\bigcup_{j}\mathbf{e}_{j}
to
y_{0}
is a based-homotopy equivalence

Proof.

Let
\mu:X\to Y
be the map whose restriction to
X_{j}
is
\mu_{j,1}
. Let
K_{j}=\zeta_{j}\circ\mu_{j}:X_{j}\times I\to X_{j}
and define
K:X\times I\to X
so the restriction to
X_{j}\times I
is
K_{j}
. Since each
K_{j}
is the constant homotopy at the basepoint,
K
is well-defined. Since the projection
R_{k}\circ K=\bigvee_{j=1}^{k}K_{j}:X\times I\to X_{\leq k}
is continuous for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
K
is continuous. By construction,
K
is a homotopy from
id_{X}
to
\zeta\circ\mu
. Next, let
L_{j}:Y_{j}\times I\to Y_{j}
be the based homotopy from
id_{Y_{j}}
to
\mu_{j,1}\circ\zeta_{j}
illustrated in Figure
4
. The analogous construction shows that one can construct a homotopy
L
from
id_{Y_{j}}
to
\mu_{j,1}\circ\zeta
using the maps
L_{j}
.
∎

Figure 5:
The based homotopy equivalence
\zeta:Y\to X
, which collapses the attached arcs.

Remark 3.2
.

The construction of the homotopy equivalence in the proof of Lemma
3.1
shows that a sequence of based homotopy equivalences
(A_{j},a_{j})\simeq(B_{j},b_{j})
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
induced a based homotopy equivalence
\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}(A_{j},a_{j})\simeq\bigcurlyvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}(B_{j},b_{j})
of the shrinking wedges. Thus, if desired one may replace each
X_{j}
with any representative of its homotopy type.

The space
Y
is a shrinking wedge of CW-complexes and so the content of Remark
2.18
applies. In particular, there are lifted maps
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}:\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k+1}\to\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
satisfying
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}\circ p_{\leq k+1}=p_{\leq k}\circ\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
.

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}}
\scriptstyle{p_{k+1}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{k}}
\textstyle{Y_{\leq k+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{k+1,k}}
\textstyle{Y_{\leq k}}

We have
\widehat{Y}=\varprojlim_{k}(\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k},\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k})
with projection maps
\widetilde{r}_{k}:\widehat{Y}\to\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
and
\widehat{Y}_{0}
is the path component of
\widehat{y}_{0}=(\widetilde{y}_{0})
in
\widehat{Y}
. Set
\widehat{p}=\varprojlim_{k}p_{\leq k}
and let
\widehat{p}_{0}:\widehat{Y}_{0}\to Y
be the restriction of
\widehat{p}
to
\widehat{Y}_{0}
. The canonical homomorphism
\phi_{Y}:\pi_{1}(Y)\to\varprojlim_{k}\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k})
,
\phi(\alpha)=((r_{k})_{\#}(\alpha))
is injective.

There also exists a generalized universal covering map
p:\widetilde{Y}\to Y
where
\widetilde{Y}
has the standard construction (Definition
2.8
). The lifting property of
p_{\leq k}
gives an induced map
\varrho_{k}:\widetilde{Y}\to\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
,
\varrho_{k}([\ell])=[r_{k}\circ\ell]
. The canonical map
\phi_{Y}:\widetilde{Y}\to\widehat{Y}_{0}
,
\phi_{Y}(\alpha)=(\varrho_{k}(\alpha))
is a continuous bijection, which satisfies
\widetilde{r}_{k}\circ\phi_{Y}=\varrho_{k}
. The coreflection
\phi_{Y}:\widetilde{Y}\to\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Y}_{0})
is a homeomorphism.

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{Y}}
\textstyle{\widehat{Y}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{r}_{k}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}}

By Corollary
2.17
, the based homotopy equivalence
\zeta:Y\to X
lifts to a based homotopy equivalence
\widetilde{\zeta}:\widetilde{Y}\to\widetilde{X}
satisfying
q\circ\widetilde{\zeta}=\zeta\circ p
. Similarly, the homotopy equivalence
\zeta_{\leq k}=\bigvee_{j=1}^{k}\zeta_{j}:Y_{\leq k}\to X_{\leq k}
lifts to based homotopy equivalence
\widetilde{\zeta}_{\leq k}:\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\to\widetilde{X}_{\leq k}
such that
q_{\leq k}\circ\widetilde{\zeta}_{\leq k}=p_{\leq k}\circ\zeta_{\leq k}
. Since,
\widetilde{R}_{k+1,k}\circ\widetilde{\zeta}_{\leq k+1}=\widetilde{\zeta}_{\leq k}\circ\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
, we may take the limit
\widehat{\zeta}=\varprojlim_{k}\widetilde{\zeta}_{\leq k}:\widehat{Y}\to\widehat{X}
. By lifting the homotopy inverse for
\zeta_{\leq k}
and the relevant homotopies, it is straightforward from taking inverse limits that
\widehat{\zeta}
is a homotopy equivalence (even though the domain and codomain are neither path connected nor locally path connected). Moreover, the restriction
\widehat{\zeta}_{0}:\widehat{Y}_{0}\to\widehat{X}_{0}
to the path component of the basepoint is also a homotopy equivalence. In summary, we have the following commutative diagram where the vertical maps are homotopy equivalences.

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\zeta}}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{Y}}
\textstyle{\widehat{Y}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\zeta}_{0}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\widetilde{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{X}}
\textstyle{\widehat{X}_{0}}

The above shows that we may replace
X
with
Y
,
\widetilde{X}
with
\widetilde{Y}
, and
\widehat{X}_{0}
with
\widehat{Y}_{0}
without any loss of homotopical or shape-theoretic information. The arcs in
\widetilde{Y}
will provide “extra space” for performing suitable deformations of
\widetilde{Y}
that are not possible in
\widetilde{X}
.

Remark 3.3
.

[Metrizability]
In general, CW-complexes are not metrizable. Consequently,
\widetilde{X}
and
\widetilde{Y}
will not always be metrizable. However, when each
X_{j}
is locally finite, each
X_{j}
is metrizable. Consequently,
Y_{j}
and the universal covers
\widetilde{X}_{j}
and
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
are metrizable. Since limits of inverse sequences of metrizable spaces are metrizable,
\widehat{X}
,
\widehat{Y}
and the subspaces
\widehat{X}_{0}
,
\widehat{Y}_{0}
will be metrizable. Finally, it is known that
\operatorname{lpc}
preserves metrizability. Therefore,
\widetilde{X}
and
\widetilde{Y}
are metrizable whenever each
X_{j}
is locally finite.

Even if some
X_{j}
are not metrizable,
\widetilde{X}
and
\widetilde{Y}
are still highly structured. Indeed, every compact subset of a CW-complex is metrizable. Combining this with the arguments used in the previous paragraph, it follows that all compact subspaces of
\widetilde{X}
,
\widetilde{Y}
,
\widehat{X}
, and
\widehat{Y}
are metrizable.

3.2
Collapsing maximal trees
T_{j}\subseteq\widetilde{X}_{j}

Since
\widetilde{X}_{j}
is a CW-complex, we may fix a maximal tree
T_{j}
in the
1
-skeleton of
\widetilde{X}_{j}
. Since
(\widetilde{X}_{j},T_{j})
has the homotopy extension property, the map
\widetilde{X}_{j}\to C_{j}=\widetilde{X}_{j}/T_{j}
that collapses
T_{j}
to a point is a homotopy equivalence. Let
c_{j}
be the image of
T_{j}
in
C_{j}
. Now,
C_{j}
is a simply connected CW-complex with a single 0-cell
c_{j}
and
\pi_{n}(C_{j})\cong\pi_{n}(X_{j})
for
n\geq 2
.

The inclusion
X_{j}\to Y_{j}
induces an embedding
\widetilde{X}_{j}\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}
in the following way. Recall that
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
is defined to be the space of path-homotopy classes of paths in
Y_{j}
starting at
y_{j}
. Let
\tau_{j}:I\to Y_{j}
,
\tau_{j}(t)=(x_{j},1-t)
be the path from
y_{j}
to
x_{j}
that parameterizes the arc
\bf{e}_{j}
and define
\tau_{j,s}:I\to Y_{j}
by
\tau_{j,s}(t)=\tau_{j}(st)
for each
s\in I
. We will also use the symbols
\tau_{j}
and
\tau_{j,s}
to denote the path-homotopy classes so that
\tau_{j}=[\tau_{j}]
and
\tau_{j,s}=[\tau_{j,s}]
.

•

We identify
\widetilde{X}_{j}
with the subspace
\{\tau_{j}\delta\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}\mid\delta\in\widetilde{X}_{j}\}
of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
by the closed embedding
\widetilde{X}_{j}\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}
,
\delta\mapsto\tau_{j}\delta
. Under this identification,
\widetilde{x}_{j}=\tau_{j}
is the basepoint of
\widetilde{X}_{j}
.

•

for each
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
,
\mathbf{e}_{j,\beta}=\{\beta\tau_{j,s}\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}\mid s\in I\}
is an arc.

•

for
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
,
\widetilde{x}_{j,\beta}=\beta\tau_{j}
is the point where the arc
\mathbf{e}_{j,\beta}
meets
\widetilde{X}_{j}
and
\widetilde{y}_{j,\beta}=\beta
is the free endpoint of
\mathbf{e}_{j,\beta}
. Note that
\widetilde{y}_{j}=\widetilde{y}_{j,1}
is the basepoint of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
. We refer to the points
\widetilde{y}_{j,\beta}
as the
arc-endpoints
of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
.

•

\mathbf{T}_{j}=T_{j}\cup\bigcup_{\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})}\mathbf{e}_{j,\beta}
is a maximal tree in
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
.

In summary, the space
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
consists of the subspace
\widetilde{X}_{j}
with an arc
\mathbf{e}_{j,\beta}
attached at
\beta\tau_{j}\in p_{j}^{-1}(x_{j})
for each
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
.

Figure 6:
The structure of the universal covering map
p_{j}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\to Y_{j}
where the subspace
\widetilde{X}_{j}
is illustrated as a disk. The arc-endpoints form the fiber
p_{j}^{-1}(y_{j})
and the attachment points form the fiber
p_{j}^{-1}(x_{j})
. The path illustrated in
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
is the lift of a given
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, which can be factored as
\tau_{j}\delta\tau_{j}^{-1}
for
\delta\in\pi_{1}(X_{j})
.

We may identify the quotient space
D_{j}=\widetilde{Y}_{j}/T_{j}
with the one-point union
D_{j}=(C_{j},c_{j})\vee(E_{j},c_{j})
where
E_{j}=f_{j}(\mathbf{T}_{j})
is a wedge of arcs with the weak topology. The quotient map
f_{j}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\to D_{j}
is also a homotopy equivalence (see Figure
7
). We will also write
\mathbf{e}_{j,\beta}
to denote the arc
f_{j}(\mathbf{e}_{j,\beta})
in
E_{j}
and
\widetilde{y}_{j,\beta}
to denote its endpoint. We give a specific construction of a homotopy inverse
g_{j}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}/T_{j}\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}
of
f_{j}
since we will need for it to have special features.

Figure 7:
The quotient map
f_{j}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\to D_{j}
where
\widetilde{X}_{j}
is illustrated as a disk in the domain.

•

Since
(\widetilde{Y}_{j},\widetilde{X}_{j})
has the homotopy extension property, there is a retraction
h_{1}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times\{0\}\cup\widetilde{X}_{j}\times I
. Instead of using an arbitrary retraction, we choose
h_{1}
so that

–

if
\mathbf{a}_{j,\beta}=\{\gamma\tau_{j,s}\mid s\in[0,1/3]\}
is the third of
\mathbf{e}_{j,\beta}
containing the arc-endpoint
\widetilde{y}_{j,\beta}
, then
h_{1}
projects
\mathbf{a}_{j,\beta}\times I
vertically onto
\mathbf{a}_{j,\beta}\times\{0\}
,

–

h_{1}(\mathbf{e}_{j,\beta}\times I)\subseteq(\mathbf{e}_{j,\beta}\times\{0\})\cup(\widetilde{x}_{j,\beta}\times I)
.

•

Since
(\widetilde{X}_{j},T_{j})
has the homotopy extension property, there is a retraction

h_{2}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times\{0\}\cup\widetilde{X}_{j}\times I\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times\{0\}\cup T_{j}\times I
such that
h_{2}(\widetilde{X}_{j}\times I)\subseteq\widetilde{X}_{j}\times\{0\}\cup T_{j}\times I
.

•

Define
h_{3}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times\{0\}\cup T_{j}\times I\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}
to be the identity on
\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times\{0\}
and, on
T_{j}\times I
, to be a choice of contraction
T_{j}\times I\to T_{j}
for
T_{j}
(there will be no benefit to choosing this to be a based contraction of
T_{j}
).

Let
H_{j}=h_{3}\circ h_{2}\circ h_{1}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}
(see Figure
8
). The map
H_{j}(y,1):\widetilde{Y}_{j}\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}
is constant on
T_{j}
and thus induces a unique map
g_{j}:D_{j}\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}
satisfying
g_{j}\circ f_{j}(y)=H_{j}(y,1)
. By construction,
H_{j}
is a homotopy from
id_{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
to
g_{j}\circ f_{j}
. Because we require
H_{j}(T_{j}\times I)\subseteq T_{j}
,
H_{j}
cannot be constructed as a lift of the homotopy
Y_{j}\times I\to Y_{j}
used in Section
3.1
(recall Figure
4
).

Figure 8:
The homotopy
H_{j}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}
constructed as a composition. There is a square attached to
\widetilde{X}_{j}\times I
(represented by the cylinder), at each vertex of
T_{j}
; however, this is not reflected in this illustration for the sake of clarity.
T_{j}\times I
is represented by the shaded gray surface.

The map
f_{j}\circ H_{j}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I\to D_{j}
sends
T_{j}\times I
to the point
c_{j}
and so there is a unique map
G_{j}:D_{j}\times I\to D_{j}
making the following diagram commute.

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{j}\times id}
\scriptstyle{H_{j}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{j}}
\textstyle{D_{j}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G_{j}}
\textstyle{D_{j}}

Since
G_{j}(f_{j}(y),1)=f_{j}\circ H_{j}(y,1)=f_{j}\circ g_{j}\circ f_{j}(y)
where
f_{j}
is surjective, it follows that
G_{j}(d,1)=f_{j}\circ g_{j}(d)
. Thus
G_{j}
is a homotopy from
id_{D_{j}}
to
f_{j}\circ g_{j}
.

While the construction of
f_{j}
,
g_{j}
,
H_{j}
, and
G_{j}
is mostly standard (
[
11
, Prop. 0.17]
), our choice of
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
ensure the following important features.

(1)

H_{j}
and
G_{j}
are the constant homotopies on a uniform neighborhood of every arc-endpoint,

(2)

f_{j}(\widetilde{X}_{j})\subseteq C_{j}
and
g_{j}(C_{j})\subseteq\widetilde{X}_{j}
,

(3)

H_{j}(\widetilde{X}_{j}\times I)\subseteq\widetilde{X}_{j}
and
G_{j}(C_{j}\times I)\subseteq C_{j}
,

(4)

f_{j}(\mathbf{T}_{j})\subseteq E_{j}
and
g_{j}(E_{j})\subseteq\mathbf{T}_{j}
,

(5)

H_{j}(\mathbf{T}_{j}\times I)\subseteq\mathbf{T}_{j}
and
G_{j}(E_{j}\times I)\subseteq E_{j}
.

Note that (2) and (3) imply that the restricted maps
(f_{j})|_{\widetilde{X}_{j}}:\widetilde{X}_{j}\to C_{j}
and
(g_{j})|_{C_{j}}:C_{j}\to\widetilde{X}_{j}
are homotopy inverses. Similarly, (4) and (5) imply that
(f_{j})|_{\mathbf{T}_{j}}:\mathbf{T}_{j}\to E_{j}
and
(g_{j})|_{E_{j}}:E_{j}\to\mathbf{T}_{j}
are homotopy inverses.

3.3
Quotients of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
by translates of
T_{j}

The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
acts on
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
by deck transformation:
\Delta_{\beta}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}
,
\Delta_{\beta}(\alpha)=\beta\alpha
,
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Note that
\Delta_{\beta}
maps
m
-cells to
m
-cells,
\Delta_{\beta}(\widetilde{X}_{j})=\widetilde{X}_{j}
, and that
\Delta_{\beta}
permutes the discrete set of arc-endpoints
\{\widetilde{y}_{j,\gamma}\mid\gamma\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})\}
. In particular, the translated trees
\beta T_{j}=\Delta_{\beta}(T_{j})
and
\beta\mathbf{T}_{j}=\Delta_{\beta}(\mathbf{T}_{j})
are maximal trees in the 1-skeleton of
\widetilde{X}_{j}
and
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
respectively. Later on, we will need to consider quotients of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
by arbitrary translates of the tree
\beta T_{j}
. For this purpose, we establish notation for the corresponding homotopy equivalences.

Let
D_{j,\beta}=\widetilde{Y}_{j}/\beta T_{j}
be the quotient of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
obtained by collapsing
\beta T_{j}
to a point. If
f_{j,\beta}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\to D_{j,\beta}
is the quotient map, then there is a unique homomorphism
\delta_{\beta}:D_{j}\to D_{j,\beta}
such that the left square in the diagram below commutes. Define
g_{j,\beta}:D_{j,\beta}\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}
by
g_{j,\beta}=\Delta_{\beta}\circ g_{j}\circ\delta_{\beta}^{-1}
so the square on the right commutes.

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{\beta}}
\textstyle{D_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\beta}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{\beta}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{j,\beta}}
\textstyle{D_{j,\beta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{j,\beta}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}

Since the vertical maps are homeomorphisms,
f_{j,\beta}
and
g_{j,\beta}
are homotopy equivalences. To verify that these are homotopy inverses of each other, we construct the
\beta
-translates of
H_{j}
and
G_{j}
. Set

(1)

H_{j,\beta}=\Delta_{\beta}\circ H_{j}\circ(\Delta_{\beta}^{-1}\times id_{I})
,

(2)

G_{j,\beta}=\delta_{\beta}\circ G_{j}\circ(\delta_{\beta}^{-1}\times id_{I})
.

We now have maps
H_{j,\beta}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}
and
G_{j,\beta}:D_{j}\times I\to D_{j}
that make the left and right faces of the following cube commute. The top face commutes by the definition of
G_{j}
. The front and back faces commute by the definition of
\widetilde{f}_{j,\beta}
. Since the vertical maps are homeomorphisms, the bottom face commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j,\beta}\times I}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{j}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\beta}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j,\beta}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{j,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{j,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\beta}}

(C1)

A straightforward check shows that
H_{j,\beta}
is a homotopy from
id_{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
to
g_{j,\beta}\circ f_{j,\beta}
and
G_{j,\beta}
is a homotopy from
id_{D_{j}}
to
f_{j,\beta}\circ g_{j,\beta}
.

Note that we have an analogous wedge point
c_{j,\beta}=f_{j,\beta}(\beta T_{j})=\delta_{\beta}(c_{j})
and subspaces
C_{j,\beta}=\delta_{\beta}(C_{j})
and
E_{j,\beta}=\delta_{\beta}(E_{j})
from which we have the decomposition
D_{j,\beta}=(C_{j,\beta},c_{j,\beta})\vee(E_{j,\beta},c_{j,\beta})
. Finally, we point out that the
\beta
-translated homotopies
H_{j,\beta}
and
G_{j,\beta}
enjoy the following properties just like the original maps
H_{j}
and
G_{j}
.

(1)

H_{j,\beta}
and
G_{j,\beta}
are the constant homotopies on a uniform neighborhood of every arc-endpoint,

(2)

f_{j,\beta}(\widetilde{X}_{j})\subseteq C_{j,\beta}
and
g_{j,\beta}(C_{j,\beta})\subseteq\widetilde{X}_{j}
,

(3)

H_{j,\beta}(\widetilde{X}_{j}\times I)\subseteq\widetilde{X}_{j}
and
G_{j,\beta}(C_{j,\beta}\times I)\subseteq C_{j,\beta}
,

(4)

f_{j,\beta}(\beta\mathbf{T}_{j})\subseteq E_{j,\beta}
and
g_{j,\beta}(E_{j,\beta})\subseteq\beta\mathbf{T}_{j}
,

(5)

H_{j,\beta}(\beta\mathbf{T}_{j}\times I)\subseteq\beta\mathbf{T}_{j}
and
G_{j,\beta}(E_{j,\beta}\times I)\subseteq E_{j,\beta}
.

Note that (2) and (3) imply that the restricted maps
(f_{j,\beta})|_{\widetilde{X}_{j}}:\widetilde{X}_{j}\to C_{j,\beta}
and
(g_{j,\beta})|_{C_{j,\beta}}:C_{j,\beta}\to\widetilde{X}_{j}
are homotopy inverses. Similarly, (4) and (5) imply that
(f_{j,\beta})|_{\beta\mathbf{T}_{j}}:\beta\mathbf{T}_{j}\to E_{j,\beta}
and
(g_{j,\beta})|_{E_{j,\beta}}:E_{j,\beta}\to\beta\mathbf{T}_{j}
are homotopy inverses.

Remark 3.4
.

For
\beta,\gamma\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, we also have a canonical homeomorphism
D_{j,\beta}\to D_{j,\gamma\beta}
. Formally, this map is
\delta_{\gamma\beta}\circ\delta_{\beta}^{-1}
, however, since it is determined by left multiplication by
\gamma
, we will also denote it by
\delta_{\gamma}
. With this definition, the following diagram commutes for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\beta,\gamma\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
.

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{j,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{\gamma}}
\textstyle{D_{j,\beta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\delta_{\gamma}}

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}

4
The inverse limits
\widehat{Y}
and
\widehat{Z}

Since
\pi_{n}(X)\cong\pi_{n}(\widetilde{X})\cong\pi_{n}(\widetilde{Y})
for all
n\geq 2
, we wish to analyze the homotopical structure of
\widetilde{Y}
. However, directly verifying the continuity of the desired deformations of
\widetilde{Y}
appears to be exceptionally tedious. Thus we seek a detailed description of the inverse system
(\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k},\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k})
.

4.1
The behavior of
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}:\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k+1}\to\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the universal covering space
\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
is a CW-complex which is the union of homeomorphic copies of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
attached to each other in a tree-like fashion. We establish the following notation to keep track of the exact location of such subspaces. Recall that
\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
is the set of path-homotopy classes of paths in
\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
starting at
\widetilde{y}_{0}
and the covering map
p_{\leq k}:\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\to Y_{\leq k}
is the endpoint projection so if
\alpha=[a]
, then
p_{k}(\alpha)=a(1)
.

Definition 4.1
.

Fix
1\leq j\leq k
and let
\alpha:I\to Y_{\leq k}
be a reduced loop based at
y_{0}
. We say that
\alpha
is
non-
Y_{j}
-terminal
if either
\alpha
is constant or if for the maximal element
(a,b)\in\overline{\alpha}
, the loop
\alpha|_{[a,b]}
has image in
\bigcup_{i\neq j}Y_{i}
. Let
\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}\subseteq\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k})
denote the subset of homotopy classes of non-
Y_{j}
-terminal reduced loops.

An element
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
corresponds to a uniquely to a reduced word
w_{\alpha}
in the free product
\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k})=\ast_{j=1}^{k}\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, which does not terminate in a letter from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Since for every
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k})
, there exists some
j\in\mathbb{N}
for which
w_{\alpha}
does not end in a letter from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, we have
p_{\leq k}^{-1}(y_{0})=\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k})=\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
.

When
j\leq k
, the coset projection
\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k})\to\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k})/\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
restricts to a bijection
\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}\to\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k})/\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, so the elements of
\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
are simply a canonical choice of representatives of the elements of
\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k})/\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Moreover,
\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
indexes the set of connected components of
p_{\leq k}^{-1}(Y_{j})
in the following way: each connected component of
p_{\leq k}^{-1}(Y_{j})
is a set of the form
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}=\{\alpha\beta\in\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\mid\beta\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}\}
for some unique
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and is homeomorphic to
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
. In particular, there is a canonical homeomorphism
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}:\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}
given by
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}(\alpha\beta)=\beta
, which makes the following triangle commute.

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{p_{\leq k}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{j}}
\textstyle{Y_{j}}

Proposition 4.2
.

\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
is a CW complex, which has the weak topology with respect to the subcomplexes
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}
,
1\leq j\leq k
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
.

Remark 4.3
.

Generally we use “tilde” notation
\widetilde{X}
to indicate that Definition
2.8
is being applied and so we create a slight inconsistency with our notation for
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{k,j,\alpha}
. However, this notation most suitably reflects the fact that these two spaces are homeomorphic copies of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
and
\widetilde{X}_{j}
respectively.

Allowing
k
to vary, note that the map
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
collapses each subspace
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,k+1,\alpha}
of
\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k+1}
to a point. Additionally,
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
folds the subspaces
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
(for fixed
j\leq k
) onto each other homeomorphically in a way that reflects word reduction in
\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k})
. This folding is non-trivial because the homomorphism
(r_{k+1,k})_{\#}:\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k+1})\to\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k})
, which deletes letters from
\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k+1})
need not map
\mathbf{nt}_{k+1,j}
into
\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
. Indeed, if
1\neq\beta_{k+1}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k+1})
and
1\neq\beta_{j}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
for
j<k+1
, then
\beta_{j}\beta_{k+1}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k+1,j}
but
(r_{k+1,k})_{\#}(\beta_{j}\beta_{k+1})=\beta_{j}\notin\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
. We formalize this in the next remark.

Remark 4.4

(Behavior of
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
)
.

Fix
1\leq j\leq k+1
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k+1,j}
.

•

If
j=k+1
, then
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
maps
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
to the single point
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\alpha)
.

•

If
1\leq j\leq k
, we write
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\alpha)=\alpha^{\prime}\gamma
for unique
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. In this case,
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
maps
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically onto
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
by
\alpha\beta\mapsto\alpha^{\prime}\gamma\beta
for
\beta\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}
. In other words, if
\Delta_{\gamma}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}
is the deck transformation
\Delta_{\gamma}(\beta)=\gamma\beta
, then the following diagram of homeomorphisms commutes.

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{\gamma}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}

Indeed, because
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
\gamma\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, we have
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}(\alpha^{\prime}\gamma\beta)=\gamma\beta
.
Note that
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\alpha)\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}

\Leftrightarrow

\gamma=1

\Leftrightarrow

\Delta_{\gamma}=id_{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
.

4.2
The quotient maps
\widetilde{f}_{k}:\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\to Z_{k}

For
1\leq j\leq k
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
, let

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{k,j,\alpha}=\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}^{-1}(\widetilde{X}_{j})=\{\alpha\tau_{j}\delta\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\mid\delta\in\widetilde{X}_{j}\}

be the copy of
\widetilde{X}_{j}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}
. We will use the fixed tree
T_{j}\subseteq\widetilde{Y}_{j}
and its translates
\beta T_{j}
,
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
to define a tree
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha}
in the 1-skeleton of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{k,j,\alpha}
. Naively, one could attempt to define such a tree as
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}^{-1}(T_{j})
. However, we wish for these trees to be coherent with bonding maps
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
and the folding behavior of this map encountered in the previous section suggests that such a choice would ultimately fail. Although our choices will be entirely determined by our initial choices of
T_{j}
in
\widetilde{X}_{j}
, we must construct them by induction on
k
.

For
k=j=1
, we have
\widetilde{Y}_{1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{1,1,1}
and
\Lambda_{1,1,1}=id_{\widetilde{Y}_{1}}
. Thus, we define
\mathcal{T}_{1,1,1}=T_{1}
and set
\mathscr{T}_{1}=\{\mathcal{T}_{1,1,1}\}
.

Suppose we have defined every element of
\mathscr{T}_{k}=\{\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha}\mid 1\leq j\leq k,\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}\}
so that each
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha}
is a maximal tree in the 1-skeleton of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{k,j,\alpha}
.

Case I:
If
j=k+1
, define
\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha}=\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}^{-1}(T_{j})
.

Case II:
If
1\leq j\leq k
, write
(r_{k+1,k})_{\#}(\alpha)=\alpha^{\prime}\gamma
for
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. The tree
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
is defined in
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
by our induction hypothesis. Therefore, we set

\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha}=\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}^{-1}\circ\Delta_{\gamma}^{-1}\circ\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}})

Set
\mathscr{T}_{k+1}=\{\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha}\mid 1\leq j\leq k+1,\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k+1,j}\}
. This completes the induction.

Remark 4.5

(Coherence of trees)
.

The inductive construction of
\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
was given precisely to match with the bonding maps
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
.

Case I:
If
j=k+1
, then
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha})=\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\alpha)
and
\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha})=T_{j}
.

Case II:
If
1\leq j\leq k
and
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\gamma
are as above then
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
maps
\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically onto
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
. Moreover, if
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}})=\beta T_{j}
, then the above definition ensures

\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha})=\gamma^{-1}\beta T_{j}.

Our inductive construction of
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha}
implies that this tree will always correspond to some translation
\beta T_{j}
under the “bookkeeping” homeomorphism
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}
(this is stated formally in the next proposition). We will see later on that the homomorphisms
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}
are precisely the bridge required to witness the eventual stabilization of certain sequences of trees
\{\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}}\}
where
j
is fixed and
k\to\infty
.

Proposition 4.6
.

For every
1\leq j\leq k<\infty
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
,
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha})=\beta T_{j}
in
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
for some
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
.

Let
Z_{k}
be the quotient space of
\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
obtained by identifying each tree
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha}\in\mathscr{T}_{k}
to a point and
\widetilde{f}_{k}:\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\to Z_{k}
be the quotient map. Let
z_{k}=\widetilde{f}_{k}(\widetilde{y}_{0})
be the basepoint of
Z_{k}
. Since
\mathscr{T}_{k}
consists of a collection of disjoint, contractible subcomplexes in
\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
, it is clear that quotient map
\widetilde{f}_{k}
is a homotopy equivalence of CW-complexes. However, we wish to choose homotopy inverses for
\widetilde{f}_{k}
in a coherent way. Toward this end, we first show that the spaces
Z_{k}
are part of a uniquely determined inverse system.

Lemma 4.7
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a unique map
\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}:Z_{k+1}\to Z_{k}
such that the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{r}_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{k+1}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{k}}
\textstyle{Z_{k+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{s}_{k+1}}
\textstyle{Z_{k}}

Proof.

Since
\widetilde{f}_{k+1}
collapses each tree
\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
to a point, it suffices to show that
\widetilde{f}_{k}\circ\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
also collapses
\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
to a point. If
j=k+1
, then
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
maps
\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
to
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\alpha)
and the conclusion is clear. Suppose
1\leq j\leq k
and
(r_{k+1,k})_{\#}(\alpha)=\alpha^{\prime}\gamma
for
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. By construction,
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
maps
\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically onto
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
. Since
\widetilde{f}_{k}
maps
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
to a point, the conclusion follows.
∎

Definition 4.8
.

Let
\widehat{Z}=\varprojlim_{k}(Z_{k},\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k})
be the inverse limit with basepoint
\widehat{z}_{0}=(z_{k})
and projection maps
\widetilde{s}_{k}:\widehat{Z}\to Z_{k}
. Additionally, let
\widehat{f}=\varprojlim_{k}\widetilde{f}_{k}:(\widehat{Y},\widehat{y}_{0})\to(\widehat{Z},\widehat{z}_{0})
be the inverse limit map.

Working toward the construction of a homotopy inverse of
\widehat{f}
, we now construct a specific, coherent system of homotopy inverses
\{\widetilde{g}_{k}\}
for the sequence
\{\widetilde{f}_{k}\}
. Fix
1\leq j\leq k
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
, and set
\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}=\widetilde{f}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha})
. Notice that
Z_{k}
is a CW-complex with the weak topology with respect the set of subcomplexes of the form
\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}
. Let
\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}:\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\to\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}
be the quotient map, which is the restriction of
\widetilde{f}_{k}
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}
. Recall that
\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}
collapses
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha}
to a point and, by Proposition
4.6
,
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha}
corresponds to some translation
\beta T_{j}
in
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
, i.e.
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha})=\beta T_{j}
. In Section
3.2
, we let
\widetilde{f}_{j,\beta}:\widetilde{Y}_{j}\to D_{j,\beta}
be the quotient map collapsing
\beta T_{j}
to a point. These observations make the next proposition immediate.

Proposition 4.9
.

Suppose
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha})=\beta T_{j}
for
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Then there is a canonical homeomorphism
\lambda_{k,j,\alpha}:\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}\to D_{j,\beta}
that makes the following square commute.

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{j,\beta}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{D_{j,\beta}}

We use the homeomorphisms
\lambda_{k,j,\alpha}
to maintain track of what the maps
\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}
do to the spaces
\mathcal{D}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
when
j\leq k
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Fix
1\leq j<k+1
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k+1,j}
. Suppose

•

(r_{k+1,k})_{\#}(\alpha)=\alpha^{\prime}\gamma
for
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})

•

and
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}})=\beta T_{j}
for
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
.

Then the following cube commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j,\gamma^{-1}\beta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j,\gamma^{-1}\beta}}

(C2)

Proof.

Recall from Remark
4.5
that
\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha})=\gamma^{-1}\beta T_{j}
. Commutativity of the top face was verified in Section
4.1
. The left and right faces commute by Proposition
4.9
. The front face commutes is a special case of the left square in Remark
3.4
. The setup of the lemma is precisely the situation where
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
maps
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically to
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
. Therefore, the back face commutes by the definition of
\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}
(recall Lemma
4.7
). Since the vertical maps are surjective and all other faces commute, the bottom face commutes.
∎

4.3
A homotopy inverse
\widetilde{g}_{k}
for
\widetilde{f}_{k}

In Section
3.3
, we fixed a homotopy inverse
g_{j,\beta}:D_{j,\beta}\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}
of
f_{j,\beta}
and homotopies
H_{j,\beta}
and
G_{j,\beta}
. Using these pre-defined structures, we now construct maps
\widetilde{g}_{k}:Z_{k}\to\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
inductively as follows.

When
k=1
, we have
\widetilde{Y}_{1}=\widetilde{Y}_{j}
,
Z_{1}=D_{1}
and
\widetilde{f}_{1}=f_{1}
. Thus we define
\widetilde{g}_{1}=g_{1}
. For our induction hypothesis, we suppose that
\widetilde{g}_{k}
has been defined so that for all
1\leq j\leq k
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
,
\widetilde{g}_{k}(\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha})\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}
and, in particular,
\widetilde{g}_{k}
maps the arc-endpoints of
\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}
bijectively to the arc-endpoints of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}
. Let
\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}:\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}
be the corresponding restriction of
\widetilde{g}_{k}
.

Fix
1\leq j\leq k+1
. We will determine
\widetilde{g}_{k+1}
by defining its restriction to each subcomplex
\mathcal{D}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
as a map
\widetilde{g}_{k+1,j,\alpha}:\mathcal{D}_{k+1,j,\alpha}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
.

Case I:
If
j=k+1
, then
\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
was constructed so that
\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha})=T_{j}
. By Lemma
4.9
(in the case
\beta=1
), the left square below commutes. We define
\widetilde{g}_{k+1,j,\alpha}=\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}^{-1}\circ g_{j}\circ\lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}
so that the diagram on the right commutes.

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{k+1,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{j}}
\textstyle{D_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{j}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}

Case II:
Suppose
1\leq j\leq k
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k+1,j}
. Write

•
