(r_{k+1,k})_{\#}(\alpha)=\alpha^{\prime}\gamma
for
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})

•

and
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}})=\beta T_{j}
for
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
.

The restricted maps
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}:\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}\mapsto\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
and
\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}:\mathcal{D}_{k+1,j,\alpha}\mapsto\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
are homeomorphisms. Since
\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
is defined by hypothesis, we set

\widetilde{g}_{k+1,j,\alpha}=\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}}^{-1}\circ\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}\circ(\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k})|_{\mathcal{D}_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
so the the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{k+1,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}}

In both cases,
\widetilde{g}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
is continuous and maps arc-endpoints bijectively to arc-endpoints. This completes the definition of all
\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}
. We will use the next lemma to prove that
\widetilde{g}_{k}
is well-defined.

Lemma 4.11
.

If
1\leq j\leq k<\infty
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
, and
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha})=\beta T_{j}
for
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, then the following square commutes.

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{D_{j,\beta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{j,\beta}}

Proof.

Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
. We proceed by induction on
k
for
k\geq j
. In the case
k=j
,
\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}
is constructed according to Case I. In particular,
\beta=1
and the commuting diagram is precisely the definition of
\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}
. Suppose that
k\geq j
and that the diagram commutes for all
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
. Since
k+1>j
, the map
\widetilde{g}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
is constructed according to Case II. Using the notation from Case II (for
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
, and
\gamma,\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
), consider the following cube, which is the “inverse” of that in Lemma
4.10
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j,\gamma^{-1}\beta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{g}_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{j,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{j,\gamma^{-1}\beta}}

(C3)

Recall that
\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha})=\gamma^{-1}\beta T_{j}
and therefore, it suffices to show that the left face commutes. The top and bottom faces are the same as in Lemma
4.10
and still commute. The back face commutes by the definition of
\widetilde{g}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
. The commutativity of the front face is a case of the right square in Remark
3.4
. The right face commutes by our induction hypothesis. Since all of the horizontal maps are homeomorphisms, we conclude that the left face commutes.
∎

Theorem 4.12
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\widetilde{g}_{k}:Z_{k}\to\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
is well-defined and continuous. Moreover,

(1)

\widetilde{g}_{k}\circ\widetilde{f}_{k}:\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\to\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
is the identity on
p_{\leq k}^{-1}(y_{0})
,

(2)

\widetilde{f}_{k}\circ\widetilde{g}_{k}:Z_{k}\to Z_{k}
is the identity on
\widetilde{f}_{k}(p_{\leq k}^{-1}(y_{0}))
,

(3)

and
\widetilde{g}_{k}\circ\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}=\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}\circ\widetilde{g}_{k+1}
.

Proof.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
Z_{k}
has the weak topology with respect to the subcomplexes
\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}
and each
\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}
is clearly continuous, it suffices to check that
\widetilde{g}_{k}
is well defined. First, we make an observation: for any given
j
and
\alpha
recall that we have verified the commutativity of the diagram when
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha})=\beta T_{j}
.

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{j,\beta}}
\textstyle{D_{j,\beta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{j,\beta}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{j,\beta}}
\textstyle{D_{j,\beta}}

In Section
3.3
, we constructed
g_{j,\beta}
and
f_{j,\beta}
so that
g_{j,\beta}\circ f_{j,\beta}
is the identity map on the arc-endpoints of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
and
f_{j,\beta}\circ g_{j,\beta}
is the identity on the arc-endpoints of
D_{j,\beta}
. It follows from the diagram that
\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\beta}\circ\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\beta}
and
\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\beta}\circ\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\beta}
are the identities on the respective sets of arc-endpoints.

Two distinct subcomplexes of the form
\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}
(respectively
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}
) either meet at a single point or do not meet at all. Suppose
\{x\}=\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}\cap\mathcal{D}_{k,j^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}
where
\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}\neq\mathcal{D}_{k,j^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}
. We check that
\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}(x)=\widetilde{g}_{k,j^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}(x)
. By the definition of
\widetilde{f}_{k}
, we have
\widetilde{f}_{k}(y)=x
for a unique point
y
. In particular,
\{y\}=\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}
. Thus
\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}(y)=\widetilde{f}_{k,j^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}(y)=x
. Since
\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}\circ\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}
and
\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}\circ\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
are the identity on the arc-endpoints, we have

\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}(x)=\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}(\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}(y))=y=\widetilde{g}_{k,j^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}(\widetilde{f}_{k,j^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}(y))=\widetilde{g}_{k,j^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}(x).

This proves that
\widetilde{g}_{k}
is well-defined.

Now that well-definedness of
\widetilde{g}_{k}
is established, both (1) and (2) follow immediately from the fact in the first paragraph of the proof that for all
j
and
\alpha
, the compositions
\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}\circ\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}
and
\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}\circ\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}
fix their respecive arc-endpoint sets.

For (3), we verify that the following square commutes by checking that the compositions agree on
\mathcal{D}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
.

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}}
\textstyle{Z_{k+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}}
\textstyle{Z_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}_{k}}

When
1\leq j\leq k
,
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}\circ\widetilde{g}_{k+1}
and
\widetilde{g}_{k}\circ\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}
agree on
\mathcal{D}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
by definition of
\widetilde{g}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
. When
j=k+1
, we have
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}\circ\widetilde{g}_{k+1,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{D}_{k+1,j,\alpha})\subseteq\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha})=\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\alpha)
. For the other composition, we have the following,

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{k}\circ\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}(\mathcal{D}_{k+1,j,\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{k}\circ\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}\circ\widetilde{f}_{k+1}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{k}\circ\widetilde{f}_{k}\circ\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{k}\circ\widetilde{f}_{k}(\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\alpha))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\alpha)

where the last equality follows from (1).
∎

Since the maps
\widetilde{g}_{k}
agree with the bonding maps
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
and
\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}
, we define
\widehat{g}=\varprojlim_{k}\widetilde{g}_{k}:\widehat{Z}\to\widehat{Y}
to be the inverse limit map. Note that
\widehat{g}(\widehat{z}_{0})=\widehat{y}_{0}
.

4.4
Coherence of
\widetilde{f}_{k}
and
\widetilde{g}_{k}

To show the limit maps
\widehat{g}:\widehat{Z}\to\widehat{Y}
and
\widehat{f}:\widehat{Y}\to\widehat{Z}
are homotopy inverses, we construct homotopies
\widetilde{H}_{k}
from
id_{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}}
to
\widetilde{g}_{k}\circ\widetilde{f}_{k}
and
\widetilde{G}_{k}
from
id_{Z_{k}}
to
\widetilde{f}_{k}\circ\widetilde{g}_{k}
, which are coherent with the respective bonding maps.

Theorem 4.13
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist based homotopies
\widetilde{H}_{k}:\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\times I\to\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
from
id_{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}}
to
\widetilde{g}_{k}\circ\widetilde{f}_{k}
and
\widetilde{G}_{k}:Z_{k}\times I\to Z_{k}
from
id_{Z_{k}}
to
\widetilde{f}_{k}\circ\widetilde{g}_{k}
such that the following squares commute for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k+1}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{H}_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}\times id}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{H}_{k}}
\textstyle{Z_{k+1}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{G}_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}\times id}
\textstyle{Z_{k}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{G}_{k}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}}
\textstyle{Z_{k+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}}
\textstyle{Z_{k}}

Remark 4.14
.

The key to proving Theorem
4.13
is the relationship between the maps
\widetilde{f}_{k}
,
\widetilde{g}_{k}
and the previously defined maps
f_{j,\beta}
and
g_{j,\beta}
. In particular, whenever
1\leq j\leq k<\infty
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
, and
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha})=\beta T_{j}
, the following squares commute:

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{j,\beta}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{D_{j,\beta}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{D_{j,\beta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{j,\beta}}

Proof of Theorem
4.13
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We define
\widetilde{H}_{k}
and
\widetilde{G}_{k}
piecewise by defining their values on the subcomplexes
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\times I
and
\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}\times I
of the respective domains. Fix
1\leq j\leq k
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and suppose
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha})=\beta T_{j}
for
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Define maps
\widetilde{H}_{k,j,\alpha}:\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\times I\to\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}
and
\widetilde{G}_{k,j,\alpha}:\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}\times I\to\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}
so that the following squares commute:

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{H}_{k,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}\times id}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{G}_{k,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{k,j,\alpha}\times id}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H_{j,\beta}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
\textstyle{D_{j,\beta}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G_{j,\beta}}
\textstyle{D_{j,\beta}}

When the left diagram is restricted to
t=0
,
H_{j,\beta}
becomes the identity map. Therefore,
\widetilde{H}_{k,j,\alpha}
does too. Recall that
H_{j,\beta}
is a homotopy from
id_{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
to
g_{j,\beta}\circ f_{j,\beta}
. Therefore,
\widetilde{H}_{k,j,\alpha}
is a homotopy from

\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}^{-1}\circ id_{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}\circ\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}=id_{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}}

to

\displaystyle\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}^{-1}\circ(g_{j,\beta}\circ f_{j,\beta})\circ\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}^{-1}\circ g_{j,\beta}\circ\lambda_{k,j,\alpha})\circ(\lambda_{k,j,\alpha}^{-1}\circ f_{j,\beta}\circ\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}\circ\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}

where the second equality comes from the left square in Remark
4.14
. Because
G_{j,\beta}
is a homotopy from
id_{D_{j,\beta}}
to
f_{j,\beta}\circ g_{j,\beta}
, the same argument using the right square in Remark
4.14
shows that
\widetilde{G}_{k,j,\alpha}
is a homotopy from
id_{\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}}
to
\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}\circ\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}
. Additionally, since
H_{j,\beta}
and
G_{j,\beta}
are the constant homotopy on the respective sets of arc-endpoints, the same holds for
\widetilde{H}_{k,j,\alpha}
and
\widetilde{G}_{k,j,\alpha}
. This last observation ensures that
\widetilde{H}_{k}
and
\widetilde{G}_{k}
will be well-defined functions if we define
\widetilde{H}_{k}
to agree with
\widetilde{H}_{k,j,\alpha}
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}
and
\widetilde{G}_{k}
to agree with
\widetilde{G}_{k,j,\alpha}
on
\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}
(a detailed proof follows the same elementary line of argument used to prove
\widetilde{g}_{k}
is well-defined). Since
\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
has the weak topology with respect to the subcomplexes
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}
,
\widetilde{H}_{k}
is continuous. Similarly,
\widetilde{G}_{k}
is continuous.

With the definition and continuity of
\widetilde{H}_{k}
and
\widetilde{G}_{k}
established for all
k
, we fix
k
and work toward proving that the two squares in the statement of the theorem commute. Let
1\leq j\leq k+1
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k+1,j}
. We will show that
\widetilde{H}_{k}\circ(\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}\times id)
and
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}\circ\widetilde{H}_{k+1}
agree on each subcomplex
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}\times I
.

Case I:
If
j=k+1
, then
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,k,\alpha})=\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\alpha)
and so
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\widetilde{H}_{k+1}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,k,\alpha}\times I))=\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,k,\alpha})=\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\alpha)
. Moreover,

\widetilde{H}_{k}(\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,k,\alpha}\times I))=\widetilde{H}_{k}(\{\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\alpha)\}\times I)=\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\alpha)

since
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\alpha)\in p_{\leq k}^{-1}(y_{0})
and
\widetilde{H}_{k}
is the constant homotopy on
p_{\leq k}^{-1}(y_{0})
.

Case II:
Suppose
1\leq j\leq k
. Write
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\alpha)=\alpha^{\prime}\gamma
for
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. If
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}})=\beta T_{j}
, then
\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha})=\gamma^{-1}\beta T_{j}
. Since
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
maps
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically onto
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{j,k,\alpha^{\prime}}
, it suffices to show that
\widetilde{H}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}\circ(\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}\times id)=\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}\circ\widetilde{H}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
agree on
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}\times I
, i.e. that the top face of the following cube commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{k+1,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{j,\gamma^{-1}\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\gamma}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{j,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}\times id}

(C4)

The front and back faces commute by the definition of
\widetilde{H}_{k+1,j,\alpha}
and
\widetilde{H}_{k,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
. Commutativity of the right face was given in Section
4.1
and the left face follows immediately. It suffices to check the bottom face. Recall that for any
\nu\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
and
\eta\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}
, the formula for
H_{j,\nu}
is
H_{j,\nu}(\eta,t)=\nu H_{j}(\nu^{-1}\eta,t)
. Therefore, if
(\eta,t)\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I
, then

H_{j,\beta}\circ(\Delta_{\gamma}\times id)(\eta,t)=H_{j,\beta}(\gamma\eta,t)=\beta H_{j}(\beta^{-1}\gamma\eta,t)

The other direction is

\Delta_{\gamma}\circ H_{j,\gamma^{-1}\beta}(\eta,t)=\Delta_{\gamma}(\gamma^{-1}\beta H_{j,\beta}(\beta^{-1}\gamma\eta,t))=\beta H_{j,\beta}(\beta^{-1}\gamma\eta,t)

This proves the bottom square commutes. We conclude that the top square commutes, completing the proof for Case II.

The proof for the coherence of the maps
\widetilde{G}_{k}
with
\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}
is nearly identical, replacing the homomorphisms
\Lambda_{-,-,-}
with
\lambda_{-,-,-}
and
\Delta_{-}
with
\delta_{-}
so we omit the details.
∎

Theorem 4.15
.

The maps
\widehat{f}:\widehat{Y}\to\widehat{Z}
and
\widehat{g}:\widehat{Z}\to\widehat{Y}
are homotopy inverses.

Proof.

The maps
\widetilde{H}_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
form an inverse system of based homotopies.

\textstyle{\widehat{Y}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{H}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{3}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{H}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{r}_{3,2}\times id}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{2}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{H}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{r}_{2,1}\times id}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{1}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{H}_{1}}
\textstyle{\widehat{Y}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{r}_{3,2}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{r}_{2,1}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{1}}

The limit of this first system is a map
\widehat{H}:\widehat{Y}\times I\to\widehat{Y}
, which, by construction, is a based homotopy from
id_{\widehat{Y}}
to
\widehat{g}\circ\widehat{f}
. Here we are implicitly using the fact that inverse limits commute with finite products to identify the limit with
\varprojlim_{k}(\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k},\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k})\times\varprojlim_{k}(I,id)=\widehat{Y}\times I
.

Similarly, the homotopies
\widetilde{G}_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, form an inverse system of based homotopies.

\textstyle{\widehat{Z}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{G}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{3}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{G}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{s}_{3,2}\times id}
\textstyle{Z_{2}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{G}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{s}_{2,1}\times id}
\textstyle{Z_{1}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{G}_{1}}
\textstyle{\widehat{Z}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{s}_{3,2}}
\textstyle{Z_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{s}_{2,1}}
\textstyle{Z_{1}}

The limit of this second system is a map
\widehat{G}:\widehat{Z}\times I\to\widehat{Z}
, which, by construction, is a based homotopy from
id_{\widehat{Z}}
to
\widehat{f}\circ\widehat{g}
.
∎

Proposition 4.16
.

For the maps
\widehat{f}
,
\widehat{H}
, and
\widehat{G}
defined above, the following square commutes.

\textstyle{\widehat{Y}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{H}}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{f}\times id}
\textstyle{\widehat{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{f}}
\textstyle{\widehat{Z}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{G}}
\textstyle{\widehat{Z}}

Proof.

It suffices to check that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the following square commutes. Once this is established, the result follows from taking the inverse limit over
k
with the appropriate bonding maps in each position.

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{H}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{k}\times id}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{k}}
\textstyle{Z_{k}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{G}_{k}}
\textstyle{Z_{k}}

Fix
1\leq j\leq k<\infty
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
. Suppose
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha})=\beta T_{j}
for
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. We check that the two compositions agree on
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\times I
, i.e. that the top face of the following cube commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j,\beta}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}_{k,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{k,j,\alpha}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}_{k,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j,\beta}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{j,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{j,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j,\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}

(C5)

The front and back faces commute by the definition of
\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha}
. The left and right faces commute by the definitions of
\widetilde{H}_{k,j,\alpha}
and
\widetilde{G}_{k,j,\alpha}
respectively. The bottom face is the bottom face of Cube
C1
in Section
3.3
. Since the vertical maps are homeomorphisms, the top face commutes.
∎

Recall that
\widehat{Y}_{0}
is the path component of
\widehat{y}_{0}
in
\widehat{Y}
. Let
\widehat{Z}_{0}=\widehat{f}(\widehat{Y}_{0})
. Since
\widehat{Z}_{0}
is path connected and
\widehat{g}(\widehat{z}_{0})\in\widehat{Y}_{0}
, we have
\widehat{g}(\widehat{Z}_{0})\subseteq\widehat{Y}_{0}
. Thus the restrictions
\widehat{f}_{0}:\widehat{Y}_{0}\to\widehat{Z}_{0}
of
\widehat{f}
and
\widehat{g}_{0}:\widehat{Z}_{0}\to\widehat{Y}_{0}
of
\widehat{g}
are well-defined maps. A similar argument gives restricted homotopies
\widehat{H}_{0}:\widehat{Y}_{0}\times I\to\widehat{Y}_{0}
and
\widehat{G}_{0}:\widehat{Z}_{0}\times I\to\widehat{Z}_{0}
. Hence, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 4.17
.

The restricted maps
\widehat{f}_{0}:\widehat{Y}_{0}\to\widehat{Z}_{0}
and
\widehat{g}_{0}:\widehat{Z}_{0}\to\widehat{Y}_{0}
are based homotopy inverses. In particular,
\widehat{H}_{0}
is a based homotopy from
id_{\widehat{Y}_{0}}
to
\widehat{g}_{0}\circ\widehat{f}_{0}
and
\widehat{G}_{0}
is a based homotopy from
id_{\widehat{Z}_{0}}
to
\widehat{f}_{0}\circ\widehat{g}_{0}
. Moreover, these homotopies make the following square commute.

\textstyle{\widehat{Y}_{0}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{H}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{f}_{0}\times id}
\textstyle{\widehat{Y}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{f}_{0}}
\textstyle{\widehat{Z}_{0}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{G}_{0}}
\textstyle{\widehat{Z}_{0}}

Just as with
\widehat{Y}_{0}
, the space
\widehat{Z}_{0}
need not be locally path connected. In the next section we give a detailed account of the structure of
\widetilde{Y}
so that we may construct a locally path connected counterpart
Z
for
\widehat{Z}_{0}

5
The spaces
\widetilde{Y}
and
Z

5.1
The topological structure of
\widetilde{Y}

In this section, we provide a description of
\widetilde{Y}
similar to that of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
, namely a tree-like decomposition into copies of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
. Just as the copies of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
appear in
\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
according to the reduced words in
\pi_{1}(\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k})
, the copies of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
in
\widetilde{Y}
will be arranged according to the infinite word structure of
\varoast_{j}\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. The main difference between these two situations is that words in
\pi_{1}(Y)
may be indexed by an infinite linear order and thus copies of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
will appear in a corresponding manner.

Definition 5.1
.

Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\alpha:I\to Y
be a reduced loop based at
y_{0}
. We say that
\alpha
is
non-
Y_{j}
-terminal
if either the linear order
\overline{\alpha}
does not have a maximal element or if
(a,b)\in\overline{\alpha}
is maximal and
\alpha|_{[a,b]}
is a loop in
\bigcup_{i\neq j}Y_{i}
. For each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}\subseteq\pi_{1}(Y)
denote the subset of homotopy classes of non-
Y_{j}
-terminal reduced loops.

Remark 5.2
.

Just like the finite case, the set
\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}\subseteq\pi_{1}(Y)
provides a canonical choice of representatives for the coset space
\pi_{1}(Y)/\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Indeed, the projection
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(Y)/\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
restricts to a bijection
\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}\to\pi_{1}(Y)/\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
.

Moreover,
p^{-1}(y_{0})=\pi_{1}(Y)=\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
since for every
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(Y)
, the corresponding reduced word
w_{\alpha}
either has no terminal letter or does not terminate in a letter of
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
for all but one
j
.

Define the following subsets of
\widetilde{Y}
for each
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
.

•

\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\{\alpha\beta\in\widetilde{Y}\mid\beta\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}\}
,

•

\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\{\alpha\beta\in\widetilde{Y}\mid\beta\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}\backslash\pi_{1}(Y_{j})\}
,

•

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\{\alpha\beta\tau_{j}\in\widetilde{Y}\mid\beta\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}\}
.

By definition, we have
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

(1)

p^{-1}(Y_{j})
is the disjoint union of the sets
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
.

(2)

p^{-1}(Y_{j}\backslash\{y_{0}\})
is the disjoint union of the sets
\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
,

(3)

p^{-1}(X_{j})
is the disjoint union of the sets
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
.

Proof.

\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\subseteq p^{-1}(Y_{j})
is clear. Suppose
\gamma\in p^{-1}(Y_{j})
. If
\gamma\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, then
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\gamma}
. If
\gamma\notin\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, then we may write
\gamma=\alpha\beta
where
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
and
\beta\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}
, giving
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. This proves
p^{-1}(Y_{j})=\bigcup\{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}\}
.

To show the sets
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
are disjoint, suppose
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
for
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
. Write
\gamma=\alpha\beta=\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}
for paths
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}\backslash\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Now
\beta^{\prime\prime}=\beta(\beta^{\prime})^{-1}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
corresponds to a letter in the group of reduced words
\varoast_{j}\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Now
\delta=\alpha\beta^{\prime\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})^{-1}=1
in
\pi_{1}(Y)
and so
[w_{\delta}]=e
in
\varoast_{j}\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. However, this means that the unreduced word
w_{\alpha}w_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}w_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{-1}
is equivalent to the empty word. Because
w_{\alpha}
and
w_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{-1}
are already reduced, this is only possible if
[w_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}]=e
, i.e. if
\beta^{\prime\prime}=1
in
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. It follows that
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
.

(1) provides the non-trivial parts of the arguments for (2) and (3). The remainder of these proofs are straightforward.
∎

Because
Y
is locally contractible at each point of
Y\backslash\{y_{0}\}
, a straightforward argument gives the next proposition.

Proposition 5.4
.

For each
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
,

(1)

\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is open in
\widetilde{Y}
,

(2)

\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is a path component of
p^{-1}(Y_{j}\backslash\{y_{j}\})
,

(3)

p|_{\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}:\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\to Y_{j}\backslash\{y_{j}\}
is a surjective local homeomorphism.

Proposition 5.5
.

For each
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is the closure of
\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
in
\widetilde{Y}
.

Proof.

Note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
consists of
\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
and the elements
\alpha\beta
,
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Fixing
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, let
V
be a path-connected neighborhood of
y_{0}
in
Y
so that
N(\alpha\beta,V)
is a basic and path-connected neighborhood of
\alpha\beta
in
\widetilde{Y}
. Since we have assumed from the start that
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})\neq 1
,
Y_{j}
consists of more than a point and so there exists a path
\eta:(I,0)\to(V\cap Y_{j},y_{j})
with
\eta(1)\neq y_{0}
. Writing
\gamma=[\eta]
, we have
\alpha\beta\gamma\in N(\alpha\beta,V)\cap\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. This proves
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
. Since
\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
, it suffices to prove that
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is closed in
\widetilde{Y}
. Since
Y_{j}
is closed in
Y
,
p^{-1}(Y_{j})
is closed. Hence, it is enough to show that
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is closed in
p^{-1}(Y_{j})
.

Let
\gamma\in p^{-1}(Y_{j})\backslash\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. Proposition
5.3
gives that
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
where
\alpha\neq\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
. Write
\gamma=\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}
for
\beta^{\prime}\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}
.

Case I:
If
\beta^{\prime}
does not represent a loop, i.e.
\beta^{\prime}\notin\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, then by (1) of the previous proposition,
\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
is an open neighborhood of
\gamma
disjoint from
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
.

Case II:
If
\beta^{\prime}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, we may use the fact that
\widetilde{Y}
is Hausdorff to find a path-connected neighborhood
V
of
y_{0}
in
Y
such that
\alpha\notin N(\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime},V)
. Moreover, since
j
is fixed, we may choose
V
small enough so that
V\cap Y_{j}
is homeomorphic to a half-open arc in
Y_{j}\backslash X_{j}
. We claim that
N(\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime},V)\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\emptyset
. Suppose, to obtain a contradiction, that
\alpha\beta=\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\delta
for
\beta\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}
and
\delta\in\widetilde{V}
(where
V
has basepoint
y_{0}
). Note that
\delta(1)
lies in the half-open arc
Y_{j}\backslash X_{j}
. Let
\epsilon
be the homotopy class of the path
I\to Y_{j}
, which parameterize the arc from
\beta(1)=\delta(1)
to
y_{j}=y_{0}
. By our choice of
V
, we must have
\delta\epsilon\in\pi_{1}(\bigcup_{i\neq j}Y_{i},y_{0})
, that is,
w_{\delta\epsilon}
contains no letters from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
.

Recall that
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
are being represented by reduced loops such that the corresponding reduced words
w_{\alpha}
and
w_{\alpha^{\prime}}
do not end in a letter from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. If
\beta\epsilon=1
in
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, then
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\delta\epsilon
, however, this contradicts
\alpha\notin N(\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime},V)
. If
1\neq\beta\epsilon\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, then we have
\alpha\beta\epsilon=\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\delta\epsilon
in
\pi_{1}(Y)
where
\beta\epsilon\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Now,
w_{\alpha\beta\epsilon}
consists of the reduced word
w_{\alpha}
, which does not end in a letter from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, followed by the non-trivial letter
(\beta\epsilon)\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Thus
w_{\alpha\beta\epsilon}
is a reduced representative of
w_{\alpha^{\prime}}\beta^{\prime}w_{\delta\epsilon}
. However,
w_{\alpha^{\prime}}\beta^{\prime}
is reduced and
w_{\delta\epsilon}
has no letters from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. The only way for
w_{\alpha^{\prime}}\beta^{\prime}w_{\delta\epsilon}
to reduce to
w_{\alpha}(\beta\epsilon)
is if
[w_{\delta\epsilon}]=e
, that is, if
\delta\epsilon=1
. However, this would give
\alpha(\beta\epsilon)=\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}
. Since
w_{\alpha}(\beta\epsilon)=w_{\alpha^{\prime}}\beta^{\prime}
as reduced words, the terminal letters must be equal, i.e.
\beta\epsilon=\beta^{\prime}
. This implies
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
; a contradiction.
∎

Proposition 5.6
.

For each
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is a path component of
p^{-1}(Y_{j})
. Moreover,
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is locally path connected and simply connected.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma:I\to p^{-1}(Y_{j})
be a path. By Proposition
5.3
, we may assume
\Gamma(0)=\alpha\beta
for
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
and
\beta\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}
. Now
\gamma=p\circ\Gamma:I\to Y_{j}
starts at
\beta(1)
. Let
\widetilde{\gamma}_{s}(t)=\alpha\beta[\gamma_{t}]
be the standard lift as described in Remark
2.9
. Now it is clear that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{s}
is a path in
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
and by unique path-lifting, we have
\Gamma=\widetilde{\gamma}_{s}
. Moreover, since
Y_{j}
is path-connected, standard lifts may be used to show each
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is path-connected. We conclude that the sets
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
are the path components of
p^{-1}(Y_{j})
.

Next, we fix
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
and show
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is locally path connected. It is clear from (3) of Proposition
5.4
that
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is locally path connected at the points of
\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. Fix
\alpha\beta\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
for
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Let
V
be a neighborhood of
y_{0}
in
Y
such that
V\cap Y_{j}
is a half-open arc in
Y_{j}\backslash X_{j}
. We will show that
N(\alpha\beta,V)\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is path connected. Consider
\alpha\beta\gamma=\alpha\beta^{\prime}
for
\gamma\in\widetilde{V}
and
\beta^{\prime}\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}
. From this, we have
\gamma=\beta^{-1}\beta^{\prime}
. Therefore,
\gamma
represents a path in
Y_{j}\cap V
. In particular, if
\epsilon:I\to Y_{j}\cap V
parameterizes the arc from
y_{0}
to
\gamma(1)
, then the standard lift
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{s}(t)=\alpha\beta[\epsilon_{t}]
gives a path in
N(\alpha\beta,V)\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
from
\alpha\beta
to
\alpha\beta\gamma
.

Finally, suppose
\ell:I\to\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is a loop based at
\alpha
. Since
\widetilde{Y}
is simply connected,
\ell
is null-homotopic in
\widetilde{Y}
. Since
p\circ\ell
has image in
Y_{j}
and
Y_{j}
is a retract of
Y
,
p\circ\ell
is null-homotopic loop in
Y_{j}
. Let
K:(I^{2},\{0,1\}\times I\cup I\times\{1\})\to(Y_{j},y_{j})
be a null-homotopy of
p\circ\ell
. Consider the lift
\widetilde{K}:(I^{2},\{0,1\}\times I\cup I\times\{1\})\to(\widetilde{Y},\alpha)
of
K
. Since
\operatorname{Im}(\widetilde{K})
is path-connected and contains
\alpha
,
\operatorname{Im}(\widetilde{K})
must lie in the path component
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
of
p^{-1}(Y_{j})
(recall Proposition
5.4
). Unique lifting ensures that
\widetilde{K}
is a null-homotopy of
\ell
. We conclude that
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is simply connected.
∎

Theorem 5.7
.

For each
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, the restriction
p|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}:\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\to Y_{j}
is a universal covering map.

Proof.

The restriction
p|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
inherits the required lifting property of a generalized covering map from that of
p
. Proposition
5.6
ensures that
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is path connected, locally path connected, and simply connected. This fulfills all requirements for
p|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
to be a generalized universal covering map. It follows from standard covering space theory
[
18
]
that every generalized covering map where the codomain is path connected, locally path connected, and semilocally simply connected is a covering map in the usual sense. Since
Y_{j}
meets all of these conditions,
p|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
is a universal covering map.
∎

Recall that
p_{j}:(\widetilde{Y}_{j},\widetilde{y}_{j})\to(Y_{j},y_{j})
denotes the universal covering map over
Y_{j}
(also with the whisker topology construction).

Corollary 5.8
.

For each
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, the map
\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}:\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\to\widetilde{Y}_{j}
,
\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}(\alpha\beta)=\beta
is a homeomorphism satisfying
p_{j}\circ\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}=p|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}

Using the previous results for
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
and the fact that
\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
may be identified with the copy of
\widetilde{X}_{j}
in
\widetilde{Y}
, we also have the following.

Proposition 5.9
.

For each
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
,

•

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is closed in
\widetilde{Y}
,

•

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is a path component of
p^{-1}(X_{j})
,

•

The restriction
p|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\to X_{j}
is a universal covering map.

Definition 5.10
.

For each
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
and
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, define

•

\mathbf{e}_{\infty,j,\alpha,\gamma}=\{\alpha\gamma\tau_{j,s}\mid s\in I\}
,

•

\widetilde{x}_{\infty,j,\alpha,\gamma}=\alpha\gamma\tau

•

\widetilde{y}_{\infty,j,\alpha,\gamma}=\alpha\gamma

We refer to the points
\widetilde{y}_{\infty,j,\alpha,\gamma}
as the
arc-endpoints
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
.

Remark 5.11
.

Under the identification of
\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
consists of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
with an arc
\mathbf{e}_{\infty,j,\alpha,\gamma}
attached at
\widetilde{x}_{\infty,j,\alpha,\gamma}
for each
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Moreover, the subspace of arc-endpoints
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\{\widetilde{y}_{\infty,j,\alpha,\gamma}\mid\gamma\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})\}
is discrete and closed in
\widetilde{Y}
.

Recall that
\phi_{Y}:\widetilde{Y}\to\widehat{Y}_{0}
,
\phi_{Y}(\alpha)=(\varrho_{k}(\alpha))
is a continuous bijection, which need not be a homeomorphism. We end this section by showing that
\phi_{Y}
is a homeomorphism on the individual subspaces
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
of
\widetilde{Y}
.

Theorem 5.12
.

Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
. Write
\varrho_{k}(\alpha)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\gamma_{k}
for
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
\gamma_{k}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. The continuous injection
\phi_{Y}:\widetilde{Y}\to\widehat{Y}_{0}
maps
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically onto
\widehat{Y}_{0}\cap\left(\prod_{k\geq j}\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\times\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}\{\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\}\right)
.

Proof.

To simplify notation, let
A_{\infty}=\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
,
A_{k}=\{\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\}
for
1\leq j\leq k-1
and
A_{k}=\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
for
k\geq j
. We wish to show that
\phi_{Y}
maps
A_{\infty}
onto
\mathbf{A}=\widehat{Y}_{0}\cap\left(\prod_{k\geq 1}A_{k}\right)
. We first check that
\phi_{Y}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha})\subseteq\mathbf{A}
.

Suppose
\alpha\beta\in A_{\infty}
for
\beta\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}
. When
k\geq j
,
\varrho_{k}(\alpha\beta)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}(\gamma_{k}\beta)
where
\gamma_{k}\beta\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}
. Thus
\varrho_{k}(\alpha\beta)\in A_{k}
. When
1\leq k\leq j-1
, we have
\varrho_{k}(\alpha\beta)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}
. Thus
\phi_{Y}(\alpha\beta)\in\mathbf{A}
, proving the desired inclusion.

When
k\geq j
, the restricted map
\varrho_{k}:A_{\infty}\to A_{k}
is given by
\varrho_{k}(\alpha\beta)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}(\gamma_{k}\beta)
and thus the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{A_{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\varrho_{k})|_{A_{\infty}}}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{A_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{\gamma_{k}}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}

Since the veritical and bottom maps are homeomorphisms, so is the top map. Then we have a sub-inverse system
(A_{k},(\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k})|_{A_{k+1}})
of
(\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k},\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k})
with limit
\mathbf{A}=\varprojlim_{k}A_{k}
. Since the maps
(\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k})|_{A_{k+1}}
are homeomorphisms for
k\geq j
, the projection maps
(\widetilde{r}_{k})|_{\mathbf{A}}:\mathbf{A}\to A_{k}
are homeomorphisms for
k\geq j
. Thus for all
k\geq j
, the maps
(\widetilde{r}_{k})|_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}\circ(\varrho_{k})|_{A_{\infty}}:A_{\infty}\to\mathbf{A}
are homeomorphisms. Since
\widetilde{r}_{k}\circ\phi_{Y}=\varrho_{k}
, we have
\phi_{Y}|_{A_{\infty}}=(\widetilde{r}_{k})|_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}\circ(\varrho_{k})|_{A_{\infty}}
.
∎

5.2
Stabilization of trees and the quotient map
\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{Y}\to Z

In the proof of Theorem
5.12
, we observed that the “bookkeeping” homeomorphisms
\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}
allowed us to show that the maps
\varrho_{k}
,
k\geq j
send each
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically to
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
for some
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
(when
k\geq j
). The trees
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
then correspond to a sequence of trees
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}})=\beta_{k}T_{j}
in
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
. In order to make a unique choice of tree
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
that is coherent with the trees
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
, we need for the sequence
\{\beta_{k}\}
in
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
to stabilize. This stabilization is established in the next lemma.

Lemma 5.13

(Stabilization)
.

Let
1\leq j<\infty
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, and set
\alpha_{k}=\varrho_{k}(\alpha)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\gamma_{k}
for
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
\gamma_{k}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. If
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}})=\beta_{k}T_{j}
for
\beta_{k}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, then sequence
\{\beta_{k}\}_{k\geq j}
is eventually constant.

Proof.

Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
. Using the notation established in the statement of the lemma, recall that
\Lambda_{j,j,\alpha_{j}}(\mathcal{T}_{j,j,\alpha_{j}})=T_{j}
and thus
\beta_{j}=1
. Moreover, according to the inductive definition of the trees
\mathcal{T}_{-,-,-}
and the summary in Remark
4.5
, we have
\beta_{k+1}=\gamma_{k}^{-1}\beta_{k}
for all
k\geq j
. Suppose, to obtain a contradiction, that there exists
j\leq k_{1}<k_{2}<k_{3}<\cdots
such that
\beta_{k_{i+1}}\neq\beta_{k_{i}}
. Then
1\neq\gamma_{k_{i}}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. It follows that the reduced word
w_{\alpha_{k_{i}}}
corresponding to
\alpha_{k_{i}}=\alpha_{k_{i}}^{\prime}\gamma_{k_{i}}
in the free product
\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k_{i}})
terminates in a non-trivial letter from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
.

However, the word
w_{\alpha}\in\varoast_{j}\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
corresponding to
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
does not terminate in a letter from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Since some of the projection words
w_{\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
contain elements of
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
,
w_{\alpha}
must contain some letters from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. However,
w_{\alpha}
only contains finitely many letters from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
and so we may write
w_{\alpha}=w_{\alpha^{\prime}}\ell w_{\eta}
where
\ell\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
is the last appearance of a letter from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
in
w_{\alpha}
and
w_{\eta}\neq 1
has no letters from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Find
K
large enough so that
\alpha
and
\alpha_{K}=\varrho_{K}(\alpha)
have the same number of letters from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
and such that
\varrho_{K}(\eta)\neq 1
. Find
i\in\mathbb{N}
with
k_{i}>K
. Then
\varrho_{k_{i}}(\alpha)
has corresponding reduced word
\varrho_{k_{i}}(\alpha^{\prime})\ell\varrho_{k_{i}}(\eta)
where
\varrho_{k_{i}}(\eta)
is a non-trivial word in
\pi_{1}(Y_{\leq k_{i}})
with no letters from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. This contradicts the fact that
\alpha_{k_{i}}
ends in a letter from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
.
∎

Definition 5.14
.

Fix
1\leq j<\infty
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
,
\alpha_{k}
, and
\beta_{k}
as in Lemma
5.13
. We define the subspace
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
to be the tree
\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}^{-1}(\beta_{\alpha}T_{j})
where
\beta_{\alpha}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
is the eventual value of the sequence
\{\beta_{k}\}
.

Let
Z
be the quotient space of
\widetilde{Y}
where each subspace
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is collapsed to a point. Let
\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{Y}\to Z
be the quotient map and
z_{0}=\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{y}_{0})
. Since
\widetilde{Y}
is path connected and locally path connected so is
Z
. We characterize
\widetilde{f}
on the subspaces of
\widetilde{Y}
in the same way that we did for
\widetilde{f}_{k}
. Many of the proofs are diagram chases similar to those earlier and so we will omit some details.

Definition 5.15
.

For each
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, set
\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
and let
\widetilde{f}_{\infty,j,\alpha}:\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\to\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
denote the restricted quotient map of
\widetilde{f}
.

Proposition 5.16
.

Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
and suppose
\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha})=\beta_{\alpha}T_{j}
for
\beta_{\alpha}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Then there is a canonical homeomorphism
\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}:\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\to D_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}
that makes the following square commute.

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{D_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}

Proposition 5.17
.

Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, and
k\geq j
. If
\varrho_{k}(\alpha)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\gamma_{k}
for
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
\gamma_{k}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, then
\varrho_{k}(\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha})=\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Recall from the proof of Theorem
5.12
that the following diagram of homeomorphisms commutes.

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{\gamma_{k}}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{k}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}

Suppose
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}})=\beta_{k}T_{j}
for
\beta_{k}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. By Lemma
5.13
, there exists
\beta_{\alpha}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
and
K\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\beta_{k}=\beta_{\alpha}
and
\gamma_{k}=1
for all
k\geq K
. Moreover, we defined
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
so that
\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha})=\beta_{\alpha}T_{j}
. When
k\geq K
, we have

\displaystyle\varrho_{k}(\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}^{-1}(\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}^{-1}(\beta_{\alpha}T_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}^{-1}(\beta_{k}T_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}

The cases
1\leq k<K
follow directly from the case
k=K
and the equalities
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha_{k+1}^{\prime}})=\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
and
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}\circ\varrho_{k+1}=\varrho_{k}
.
∎

Lemma 5.18
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\varrho_{k}^{-1}\left(\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}\bigcup_{\beta\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}}\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\beta}\right).

Proof.

The inclusion
\subseteq
follows from Proposition
5.17
. Suppose
a\in\widetilde{Y}\backslash\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. Since
\widetilde{Y}
is the union of the subspaces
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
, we have
a\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
for some
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
. Write
\varrho_{k}(\alpha)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\gamma_{k}
for
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
\gamma_{k}\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}
. By Proposition
5.17
,
\varrho_{k}
maps
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically onto
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
and
\varrho_{k}(\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha})=\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
Thus
\varrho_{k}(a)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\backslash\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
. It follows that
a\notin\varrho_{k}^{-1}(\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\beta})
for any
1\leq j\leq k
and
\beta\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
.
∎

Theorem 5.19
.

There is a canonical, continuous bijection
\psi:Z\to\widehat{Z}_{0}
such that the following square commutes.

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{Y}}
\textstyle{\widehat{Y}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{f}_{0}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\widehat{Z}_{0}}

Proof.

To show that
\psi
is well-defined, we must show that
\widehat{f}_{0}\circ\phi_{Y}
is constant on each tree
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
. Fixing such
j
and
\alpha
, define
\alpha_{k}=\varrho_{k}(\alpha)
, and
\beta_{k}
,
k\geq j
as in Lemma
5.13
. As in the proof of that lemma, set
\alpha_{k}=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\gamma_{k}
for
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
\gamma_{k}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
so that
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\mathcal{T}_{k+1,k,\alpha_{k+1}^{\prime}})=\mathcal{T}_{k,k,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
. Recall that
\beta_{k+1}=\gamma_{k}^{-1}\beta_{k}
for all
k\geq j
. The conclusion of Lemma
5.13
is that there exists
K\geq j
such that
\beta_{k}=\beta_{\alpha}
for all
k\geq K
. Thus, for all
k\geq K
, we have
\gamma_{k}=1
and thus
\alpha_{k}=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
. It follows that
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}
maps
\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha_{k+1}}
homeomorphically onto
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}}
whenever
k\geq K
.

Recall that
\varrho_{k}
maps
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically onto
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
for all
k
. Hence, for all
k\geq K
,
\varrho_{k}
maps
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically onto
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}}
. Additionally, we have
\widetilde{f}_{k}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}})=c_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\in Z_{k}
. Thus, whenever
k+1\geq k\geq K
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}(c_{k+1,j,\alpha_{k+1}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}(c_{k+1,j,\alpha_{k+1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}(\widetilde{f}_{k+1}(\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha_{k+1}^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{k}(\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\mathcal{T}_{k+1,j,\alpha_{k+1}^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{k}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{k,j,\alpha_{k}}

Representing
\widehat{Y}
as
\varprojlim_{k\geq K}\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
and
\widehat{f}=\varprojlim_{k\geq K}\widetilde{f}_{k}
, we have

\widehat{f}\circ\phi_{Y}(\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha})=\widehat{f}\left(\prod_{k\geq K}\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}}\right)=\{(c_{k,j,\alpha_{k}})_{k\geq K}\},

which is a coherent sequence and thus represents a point in
\widehat{Z}
. This verifies that
\psi
is well-defined. Since
\widetilde{f}
is a quotient map and
\widehat{f}\circ\phi_{Y}
is continuous,
\psi
is continuous. Since
\widehat{Z}_{0}=\widehat{f}(\widehat{Y}_{0})
by definition and
\phi_{Y}
is bijection,
\widehat{f}\circ\phi_{Y}
is surjective. It follows that
\psi
is surjective.

Finally, we check that
\psi
is injective. It is enough to check that
\widetilde{f}
is constant on the fibers of
\widehat{f}_{0}\circ\phi_{Y}
. Suppose
a\neq b
in
\widetilde{Y}
and
\widehat{f}_{0}\circ\phi_{Y}(a)=\widehat{f}_{0}\circ\phi_{Y}(b)
. Write
a_{k}=\varrho_{k}(a)=a_{k}^{\prime}c_{k}
and
b_{k}=\varrho_{k}(b)=b_{k}^{\prime}d_{k}
for
a_{k}^{\prime},b_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
c_{k},d_{k}\in\widetilde{Y}_{j}
. Since
a\neq b
and
\phi_{Y}
is injective, there exists
K\in\mathbb{N}
such that
a_{k}\neq b_{k}
for all
k\geq K
. However,
\widetilde{f}_{k}(\varrho_{k}(a))=\widetilde{f}_{k}(\varrho_{k}(b))
for all
k\geq 1
and
\widetilde{f}_{k}
only identifies points in trees of the form
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha}
. Thus, for all
k\geq K
, we have
\{\varrho_{k}(a),\varrho_{k}(b)\}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{k,j_{k},\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
for some
j_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
. Specifically, we must have
a_{k}^{\prime}=b_{k}^{\prime}=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}
for
k\geq K
.

By Lemma
5.18
, we have
a\in\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
and
b\in\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j^{\prime},\beta}
for some
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
. For
k\geq K
,
\varrho_{k}
maps
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j^{\prime},\beta}
to
\mathcal{T}_{k,j_{k},\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
. Thus
j=j^{\prime}=j_{k}
for all
k\geq K
. Additionally, we must have
\varrho_{k}(\alpha)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\gamma_{k}
and
\varrho_{k}(\beta)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\delta_{k}
for
\gamma_{k},\delta_{k}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. However, Lemma
5.13
ensures that there exists
M\geq K
such that
\gamma_{k}=\delta_{k}=1
for all
k\geq M
. Thus
\varrho_{k}(\alpha)=\varrho_{k}(\beta)
for all
k\geq M
. The injectivity of
\phi_{Y}
then gives
\alpha=\beta
. Since
a,b
both lie in
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
, we have
\widetilde{f}(a)=\widetilde{f}(b)
.
∎

Definition 5.20
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\sigma_{k}:Z\to Z_{k}
be the composition
\sigma_{k}=\widetilde{s}_{k}\circ\psi
so that the following that the following diagram commutes for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{Y}}
\textstyle{\widehat{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{f}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{r}_{k}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{k}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\widehat{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{s}_{k}}
\textstyle{Z_{k}}

The proof of the next proposition follows directly from established constructions.

Proposition 5.21
.

Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, and
k\geq j
. If
\varrho_{k}(\alpha)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\gamma_{k}
for
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
, and
\gamma_{k}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, then
\sigma_{k}
maps
\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically onto
\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
and the following square commutes.

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{k}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}

Lemma 5.22
.

Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
and suppose that for all
k\geq j
, we have
\varrho_{k}(\alpha)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\gamma_{k}
for
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
\gamma_{k}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Then
\psi
maps
\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically onto
\widehat{Z}\cap\left(\prod_{k\geq j}\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\times\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}\{\widetilde{f}_{k}(\alpha_{k}^{\prime})\}\right)
.

Proof.

Consider the following commutative diagram where the right map is the restriction of
\widehat{f}_{0}
.

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widehat{Y}_{0}\cap\left(\prod_{k\geq j}\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\times\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}\{\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{f}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\widehat{Z}_{0}\cap\left(\prod_{k\geq j}\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\times\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}\{\widetilde{f}_{k}(\alpha_{k}^{\prime})\}\right)}

The left map is quotient and the top map is a homeomorphism by Theorem
5.12
. The right map in the diagram may be represented as the inverse limit
\widehat{f}_{0}=\varprojlim_{k\geq j}\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
of quotient maps. Since both sets of bonding map for this inverse system are homeomorphisms, it follows that
\widehat{f}_{0}
is a quotient map. Since
\psi
is a bijection by Theorem
5.19
, we conclude that the restriction of
\psi
in the diagram is a homeomorphism.
∎

Recall that the open sets of
\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Z}_{0})
are the path components of open sets in
\widehat{Z}_{0}
. We will use the fact that
\phi_{Y}:\widetilde{Y}\to\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Y}_{0})
is a homeomorphism, to prove the analgous fact for
Z
.

Theorem 5.23
.

\psi:Z\to\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Z}_{0})
is a homeomorphism.

Proof.

Just as we defined the open sets
\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
in
\widetilde{Y}
, define
\mathcal{U}_{k,j,\alpha}=\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\backslash\{\alpha\beta\in\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\mid\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})\}
for all
1\leq j\leq k<\infty
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
. Set
\mathcal{V}_{k,j,\alpha}=\widetilde{f}_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{k,j,\alpha})
even in the case
k=\infty
and note that
\mathcal{V}_{k,j,\alpha}
is homeomorphic to
D_{j}
without its arc-endpoints. Since
\mathcal{U}_{k,j,\alpha}
is saturated with respect to
\widetilde{f}_{k}
,
\mathcal{V}_{k,j,\alpha}
is open in
Z_{k}
. Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
. We will show that
\psi(\mathcal{V}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
is open in
\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Z}_{0})
. Consider the open sets

(1)

\mathscr{U}=\widehat{Y}_{0}\cap\left(\mathcal{U}_{j,j,\varrho_{j}(\alpha)}\times\prod_{m\neq j}\widetilde{Y}_{\leq m}\right)
in
\widehat{Y}_{0}
,

(2)

\mathscr{V}=\widehat{Z}_{0}\cap\left(\mathcal{V}_{j,j,\varrho_{j}(\alpha)}\times\prod_{m\neq j}Z_{m}\right)
in
\widehat{Z}_{0}
.

Because
\widetilde{f}_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{j,j,\varrho_{j}(\alpha)})\subset\mathcal{V}_{j,j,\varrho_{j}(\alpha)}
and
\widetilde{g}_{j}(\mathcal{V}_{j,j,\varrho_{j}(\alpha)})\subset\mathcal{U}_{j,j,\varrho_{j}(\alpha)}
, we have
\widehat{f}_{0}(\mathscr{U})\subseteq\mathscr{V}
and
\widehat{g}_{0}(\mathscr{V})\subseteq\mathscr{U}
.

Note that
\phi_{Y}^{-1}(\mathscr{U})
is the disjoint union of the path-connected open sets
\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\beta}
,
\beta\in\varrho_{j}^{-1}(\varrho_{j}(\alpha))
. Because
\phi_{Y}:\widetilde{Y}\to\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Y}_{0})
is a homeomorphism, the sets
\psi(\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\beta})
,
\beta\in\varrho_{j}^{-1}(\varrho_{j}(\alpha))
are the path components of
\mathscr{U}
.

Similarly,
\psi^{-1}(\mathscr{V})
is the disjoint union of the open sets
\mathcal{V}_{\infty,j,\beta}
for all
\beta\in\varrho_{j}^{-1}(\varrho_{j}(\alpha))
. Thus
\mathscr{V}
is the disjoint union of the path connected sets
\psi(\mathcal{V}_{\infty,j,\beta})
,
\beta\in\varrho_{j}^{-1}(\varrho_{j}(\alpha))
. If there was a path
\ell:I\to\mathscr{V}
with
\ell(0)\in\psi(\mathcal{V}_{\infty,j,\beta_{0}})
and
\ell(1)\in\psi(\mathcal{V}_{\infty,j,\beta_{1}})
for
\beta_{0}\neq\beta_{1}
in
\varrho_{j}^{-1}(\varrho_{j}(\alpha))
, then
\widehat{g}_{0}\circ\ell:I\to\mathscr{U}
would be a path in
\mathscr{U}
from a point in
\phi_{Y}(\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\beta_{0}})
to a point in
\phi_{Y}(\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\beta_{1}})
. However, this contradicts the previous paragraph. We conclude that the sets
\psi(\mathcal{V}_{\infty,j,\beta})
,
\beta\in\varrho_{j}^{-1}(\varrho_{j}(\alpha))
are the path components of
\mathscr{V}
. In particular,
\psi(\mathcal{V}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
is open in
\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Z}_{0})
.

Since
\mathcal{V}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is locally path-connected, the restriction
\psi:\mathcal{V}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\to\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Z}_{0})
is continuous. Lemma
5.22
implies that
\psi
maps
\mathcal{V}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically onto its image in
\widehat{Z}_{0}
. Therefore, if
V\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is open, then
\psi(V)
is open in the subspace
\psi(\mathcal{V}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
of
\widehat{Z}_{0}
. Since
\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Z}_{0})
has a finer topology than
\widehat{Z}_{0}
,
\psi(V)
is open in the subspace
\psi(\mathcal{V}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
of
\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Z}_{0})
. Thus
\psi:\mathcal{V}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\to\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Z}_{0})
is an open embedding. We conclude that the restriction of
\psi
on
Z\backslash\widetilde{f}(p^{-1}(y_{0}))
is an open embedding.

To complete the proof that
\psi
is a homeomorphism, we fix
\alpha\in p^{-1}(y_{0})
and set
z=\widetilde{f}(\alpha)
. It is enough to show that
\psi
maps basic neighborhoods of
z
to open sets in
\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Z}_{0})
. A basic neighborhood of
\alpha
has the form
N(\alpha,V)
where
V
is an open neigborhood of
y_{0}
in
Y
. In particular, we may assume there is a
J
such that
\bigcup_{j>J}Y_{j}\subseteq V
and
V\cap Y_{j}
is an open arc in
Y_{j}\backslash X_{j}
whenever
1\leq j\leq J
. Note that if
N(\alpha,V)
meets
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
, then
j>J
and it follows that
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\subseteq N(\alpha,V)
. Hence,
N(\alpha,V)
is saturated with respect to
\widetilde{f}
and
\widetilde{f}(N(\alpha,V))
is a basic open neighborhood of
z
in
Z
. We check that
\psi(\widetilde{f}(N(\alpha,V)))
is open in
\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Z}_{0})
.

Note that
V_{J}=V\cap Y_{\leq J}
is an open neighborhood of
y_{0}
in
Y_{\leq J}
consisting of a wedge of open arcs. Thus
N(\varrho_{J}(\alpha),V_{J})
is an open neighborhood of
\varrho_{J}(\alpha)
in
\widetilde{Y}_{\leq J}
. In fact,
p_{\leq J}
maps
N(\varrho_{J}(\alpha),V_{J})
homeomorphically onto
V_{J}
and
N(\varrho_{J}(\alpha),V_{J})
does not meet any of the trees
\mathcal{T}_{J,j,\gamma}
in
\widetilde{Y}_{\leq J}
. Thus
\widetilde{f}_{J}
maps
N(\varrho_{J}(\alpha),V_{J})
homeomorphically onto the open subset
\widetilde{f}_{J}(N(\varrho_{J}(\alpha),V_{J}))
of
Z_{J}
. Recall that we originally constructed
H_{j}
to be the constant homotopy on some neighborhood of each arc-endpoint of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
. Thus, we may choose the size of the arcs in
V\cap Y_{\leq J}
to be small enough so that
\widetilde{g}_{J}
maps
\widetilde{f}_{J}(N(\varrho_{J}(\alpha),V_{J}))
homeomorphically onto
N(\varrho_{J}(\alpha),V_{J})
. Consider the open sets

(1)

\mathscr{U}=\widehat{Y}_{0}\cap\left(N(\varrho_{J}(\alpha),V_{J})\times\prod_{k\neq J}\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\right)
in
\widehat{Y}_{0}
,

(2)

\mathscr{V}=\widehat{Z}_{0}\cap\left(\widetilde{f}_{J}(N(\varrho_{J}(\alpha),V_{J}))\times\prod_{k\neq j}Z_{k}\right)
in
\widehat{Z}_{0}
.

By our choice of
V
, we have
\widehat{f}_{0}(\mathscr{U})\subseteq\mathscr{V}
and
\widehat{g}_{0}(\mathscr{V})\subseteq\mathscr{U}
. Now
\phi_{Y}^{-1}(\mathscr{U})=\varrho_{J}^{-1}(N(\widetilde{R}_{J}(\alpha),V_{J}))
is a disjoint union of open sets of the form
N(\beta,V)
,
\beta\in\varrho_{J}^{-1}(\varrho_{J}(\alpha))
. Such sets are path connected. Therefore, the sets
\psi(N(\beta,V))
,
\beta\in\varrho_{J}^{-1}(\varrho_{J}(\alpha))
are the path components of
\mathscr{U}
.

It follows that
\psi^{-1}(\mathscr{V})=\sigma_{J}^{-1}(\widetilde{f}(N(\varrho_{J}(\alpha),V_{J})))
is the disjoint union of path connected sets of the form
\widetilde{f}(N(\beta,V))
,
\beta\in\varrho_{J}^{-1}(\varrho_{J}(\alpha))
. Then
\mathscr{V}
is the disjoint union of the path-connected sets
\psi(\widetilde{f}(N(\beta,V)))
,
\beta\in\varrho_{J}^{-1}(\varrho_{J}(\alpha))
.

Suppose that there exists a path
\ell:I\to\mathscr{V}
with
\ell(0)\in\psi(\widetilde{f}(N(\beta_{0},V)))
and
\ell(1)\in\psi(\widetilde{f}(N(\beta_{1},V)))
for
\beta_{0}\neq\beta_{1}
in
\varrho_{J}^{-1}(\varrho_{J}(\alpha))
. Then
\widehat{g}_{0}\circ\ell:I\to\mathscr{U}
is a path from a point in
N(\beta_{0},V)
to a point in
N(\beta_{1},V)
. However, this is a contradiction of the fact that
N(\beta_{0},V)
and
N(\beta_{1},V)
are distinct path components of
\mathscr{U}
. We conclude that the sets
\psi(\widetilde{f}(N(\beta,V)))
,
\beta\in\varrho_{J}^{-1}(\varrho_{J}(\alpha))
are the path components of
\mathscr{V}
. In particular,
\psi(\widetilde{f}(N(\alpha,V)))
is open in
\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Z}_{0})
.
∎

Corollary 5.24
.

Suppose
W
is locally path connected and
h:W\to Z
is a function. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

h:W\to Z
is continuous,

(2)

\psi\circ h:W\to\widehat{Z}_{0}
is continuous,

(3)

\sigma_{k}\circ h:W\to Z_{k}
is continuous for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

We will also need the following characterization of
\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}
and
\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}
.

Proposition 5.25
.

Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, and suppose that
\varrho_{k}(\alpha)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\gamma_{k}
for
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
\gamma_{k}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Then
\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\circ\varrho_{k}
and
\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\circ\sigma_{k}
for all but finitely many
k
.

Proof.

We focus on the case
k\geq j
where we may write
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}})=\beta_{k}T_{j}
. By Lemma
5.13
, we have
\beta_{k}=\beta_{\alpha}
and
\gamma_{k}=1
for all but finitely many
k
. Fix a sufficiently large
k
and consider the following cube.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j,\beta_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\gamma_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j,\beta_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\gamma_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}

(C6)

Since
\Delta_{\gamma_{k}}=id
and
\delta_{\gamma_{k}}=id
, the bottom face commutes. Commutativity of the top face is proved in Proposition
5.21
. The left face is given in Proposition
5.16
. The right face is given in Proposition
4.9
. Commutativity of the back face was verified in the proof of Theorem
5.12
. Since the diagonal maps are surjective, the front face commutes. Because
\Delta_{\gamma_{k}}=id
and
\delta_{\gamma_{k}}=id
, the front and back faces collapse into the desired triangles.
∎

5.3
A homotopy inverse
\widetilde{g}
for
\widetilde{f}

In the next theorem, we define a homotopy inverse for
\widetilde{f}
.

Theorem 5.26
.

The quotient map
\widetilde{f}:(\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{y}_{0})\to(Z,z_{0})
has a based homotopy inverse
\widetilde{g}:(Z,z_{0})\to(\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{y}_{0})
. In particular, there are homotopies
\widetilde{H}
from
id_{\widetilde{Y}}
to
\widetilde{g}\circ\widetilde{f}
and
\widetilde{G}
from
id_{Z}
to
\widetilde{f}\circ\widetilde{g}
that make the following square commute.

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{H}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}\times id}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}}
\textstyle{Z\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{G}}
\textstyle{Z}

Proof.

Recall that
\widehat{f}_{0}:\widehat{Y}_{0}\to\widehat{Z}_{0}
and
\widehat{g}_{0}:\widehat{Z}_{0}\to\widehat{Y}_{0}
are homotopy inverses. Also,
\widehat{H}:\widehat{Y}_{0}\times I\to\widehat{Y}_{0}
is a based homotopy from
id_{\widehat{Y}_{0}}
to
\widehat{g}_{0}\circ\widehat{f}_{0}
and
\widehat{G}:\widehat{Z}_{0}\times I\to\widehat{Z}_{0}
is a based homotopy from
id_{\widehat{Z}_{0}}
to
\widehat{f}_{0}\circ\widehat{g}_{0}
. Set
\widetilde{g}=\phi^{-1}\circ\widehat{g}_{0}\circ\psi:Z\to\widetilde{Y}
so that the left square commutes.

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Y}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id}
\textstyle{\widehat{Y}_{0}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Z}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{g}_{0})}
\scriptstyle{id}
\textstyle{\widehat{Z}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{g}_{0}}

Since
\phi_{Y}\circ\widetilde{g}=\widehat{g}_{0}\circ\psi:Z\to\widehat{Y}_{0}
is continuous and
Z
is locally path connected,
\widetilde{g}
is continuous by Corollary
2.19
.

Since
\operatorname{lpc}
preserves products, we have
\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Y}_{0}\times I)=\operatorname{lpc}(\widehat{Y}_{0})\times I
. Define
\widetilde{H}:\widetilde{Y}\times I\to\widetilde{Y}
by
\widetilde{H}=\phi_{Y}^{-1}\circ\widehat{H}_{0}\circ(\phi_{Y}\times id)
. The same argument used for
\widetilde{g}
gives the continuity of
\widetilde{H}
. Define
\widetilde{G}:Z\times I\to Z
by
\widetilde{G}=\psi^{-1}\circ\widehat{G}_{0}\circ(\psi\times id)
. Since
\psi\circ\widetilde{G}=\widehat{G}_{0}\circ(\psi\times id)
is continuous,
\widetilde{G}
is continuous by Corollary
5.24
. Based on the definitions given and the established results for
\widehat{H}_{0}
and
\widehat{G}_{0}
, a straightforward check shows that
\widetilde{H}
is a based homotopy from
id_{\widetilde{Y}}
to
\widetilde{g}\circ\widetilde{f}
and
\widetilde{G}
is a based homotopy from
id_{Z}
to
\widetilde{f}\circ\widetilde{g}
.

Since
\widehat{f}_{0}\circ\widehat{H}_{0}=\widehat{G}_{0}\circ(\widehat{f}_{0}\times id)
(Corollary
4.17
), a direct verification from the formulas gives
\widetilde{G}\circ(\widetilde{f}\times id)=\widetilde{f}\circ\widetilde{H}
.
∎

Remark 5.27
.

Recall that
\widehat{g}_{0}
is the restriction of
\widehat{g}=(\widetilde{g}_{k})
. Note that
\widetilde{g}
is defined precisely so that the left square in the following diagram commutes. In particular,
\psi(z)=(\sigma_{k}(z))
. Commutativity of other parts of the diagram have already been established as well.
