\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{Y}}
\textstyle{\widehat{Y}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{r}_{k}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\widehat{Z}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{g}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{s}_{k}}
\textstyle{Z_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}_{k}}

It follows that
\varrho_{k}\circ\widetilde{g}=\widetilde{g}_{k}\circ\sigma_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

We will have need to characterize the behavior of
\widetilde{g}
,
\widetilde{H}
, and
\widetilde{G}
on some relevant subspaces. We begin with
\widetilde{H}
.

Lemma 5.28
.

For all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, we have
\widetilde{H}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. Moreover, if
\widetilde{H}_{\infty,j,\alpha}:\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I\to\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is the corresponding restriction of
\widetilde{H}
, then the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{H}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}

Proof.

Write
\varrho_{k}(\alpha)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\gamma_{k}
for
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
, and
\gamma_{k}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. For brevity, write
\mathbf{A}=\widehat{Y}_{0}\cap\left(\prod_{k\geq j}\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\times\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}\{\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\}\right)
. We have already established the following.

(1)

\phi_{Y}
maps
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically onto
\mathbf{A}
,

(2)

\widehat{H}_{0}
is given by
\widetilde{H}_{k}
in each coordinate,

(3)

\widetilde{H}_{k}(\{\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\}\times I)=\{\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\}
and
\widetilde{H}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\times I)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
for all
k
.

Therefore, if
((a_{k}),t)\in\mathbf{A}\times I
, then (2) and (3) give
\widehat{H}_{0}((a_{k}),t)=(\widetilde{H}_{k}((a_{k},t)))\in\mathbf{A}
. Thus
\widehat{H}_{0}(\mathbf{A}\times I)\subseteq\mathbf{A}
. This gives

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{Y}^{-1}\circ\widehat{H}_{0}\circ(\phi_{Y}\times id)(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{Y}^{-1}\circ\widehat{H}_{0}(\mathbf{A}\times I)

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\phi_{Y}^{-1}(\mathbf{A})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}

To verify commutativity of the square, we let
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}})=\beta_{k}T_{j}
when
k\geq j
so that
\beta_{\alpha}
is the eventual value of the sequence
\{\beta_{k}\}_{k\geq j}
. Consider the following cube.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{k}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\gamma_{k}}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\gamma_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{j,\beta_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}

(C7)

Fix
k
sufficiently large so that
\beta_{k}=\beta_{\alpha}
and
\gamma_{k}=1
. For such
k
,
H_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}=H_{j,\beta_{k}}
and
\Delta_{\gamma_{k}}=id
, which makes the bottom face commute. Proposition
5.25
gives the commutativity of the left and right faces commute. The front face commutes for all
k\geq j
by the construction of
\widetilde{H}_{k}
. All of the diagonal maps are homeomorphisms. Thus the back face commutes.
∎

We characterize the behavior of
\widetilde{g}
and
\widetilde{G}
in a similar fashion.

Lemma 5.29
.

For all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, we have
\widetilde{g}(\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha})\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
and
\widetilde{G}(\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I)\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
.

Proof.

Since
\psi
is bijective, we may show
\phi_{Y}\circ\widetilde{g}(\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha})\subseteq\phi_{Y}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
. Write
\varrho_{k}(\alpha)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\gamma_{k}
for
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
, and
\gamma_{k}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. For brevity, write
\mathbf{A}=\widehat{Y}_{0}\cap\left(\prod_{k\geq j}\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\times\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}\{\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\}\right)
and
\mathbf{B}=\widehat{Z}_{0}\cap\left(\prod_{k\geq j}\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\times\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}\{\widetilde{f}_{k}(\alpha_{k}^{\prime})\}\right)
. We have already established the following:

(1)

\phi_{Y}
is a bijection and
\phi_{Y}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha})=\mathbf{A}

(2)

\psi
is a bijection and
\psi(\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha})=\mathbf{B}

(3)

\widetilde{g}_{k}(\widetilde{f}_{k}(\alpha_{k}^{\prime}))=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}
for all
k
and
\widetilde{g}_{k}(\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}})=\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
for all
k\geq j
.

Note that (3) gives
\widehat{g}_{0}(\mathbf{B})\subseteq\mathbf{A}
. Thus

\widetilde{g}(\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha})=\phi_{Y}^{-1}\circ\widehat{g}_{0}\circ\psi(\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha})=\phi_{Y}^{-1}\circ\widehat{g}_{0}\left(\mathbf{B}\right)\subseteq\phi_{Y}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{A}\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}

Similarly, the fact that
\widetilde{G}_{k}(\{\widetilde{f}_{k}(\alpha_{k}^{\prime})\}\times I)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}
for all
k
and
\widetilde{G}_{k}(\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\times I)=\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
for all
k\geq j
gives
\widehat{G}_{0}(\mathbf{B}\times I)\subseteq\mathbf{B}
. Thus
\widetilde{G}(\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I)=\psi^{-1}\circ\widehat{G}_{0}\circ(\psi\times id)(\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I)=\psi^{-1}\circ\widehat{G}_{0}\left(\mathbf{B}\times I\right)\subseteq\psi^{-1}\left(\mathbf{B}\right)=\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
.
∎

For each
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, let
\widetilde{g}_{\infty,j,\alpha}:\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
and
\widetilde{G}_{\infty,j,\alpha}:\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I\to\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
be the corresponding restrictions of
\widetilde{g}
and
\widetilde{G}
.

Proposition 5.30
.

For every
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, the following squares commute.

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{G}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times id}
\textstyle{D_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{D_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{D_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}

Proof.

Write
\varrho_{k}(\alpha)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\gamma_{k}
for
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
, and
\gamma_{k}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
and write
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}})=\beta_{k}T_{j}
. Find
k
sufficiently large so that
\beta_{\alpha}=\beta_{k}
and (using Lemma
5.25
) such that
\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\circ\varrho_{k}
and

\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\circ\sigma_{k}
. We then have that the top and bottom triangles of the following prism commute.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}

Restricting
\widetilde{g}_{k}\circ\sigma_{k}=\varrho_{k}\circ\widetilde{g}
gives the commutativity of the back face. Since
\beta_{\alpha}=\beta_{k}
, Lemma
4.11
gives the commutativity of the right face. Therefore, the left face commutes.

The second square in the proposition appears as the bottom face in the following cube.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{H}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}\times id}

(C8)

From the proof of Theorem
5.26
, we have
\widetilde{f}\circ\widetilde{H}=\widetilde{g}\circ(\widetilde{f}\times id)
, which gives the commutativity of the back face. The front face is a case of the bottom face of Cube
C1
in Section
3.3
. The right and left faces follow from Proposition
5.16
. Commutativity of the top face was proved in Lemma
5.28
. Since the vertical maps are surjective, the bottom face commutes.
∎

5.4
The topological structure of
Z

In this section, we give a more detailed topological description of the space
Z
. Recall from Section
3.3
the definition of the maximal tree
\mathbf{T}_{j}
in
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
and its translates
\beta\mathbf{T}_{j}
,
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
.

Definition 5.31
.

Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
with
j\leq k
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
. If
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha})=\beta T_{j}
, we define

(1)

\mathbf{T}_{k,j,\alpha}=\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}^{-1}(\beta\mathbf{T}_{j})
,

(2)

and set
\mathbf{T}_{k}=\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}}\mathbf{T}_{k,j,\alpha}
.

as subspaces of
\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
(
\widetilde{Y}
if
k=\infty
).

Each
\mathbf{T}_{k,j,\alpha}
consists of the maximal tree
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{k,j,\alpha}
and
\overline{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\backslash\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{k,j,\alpha}}
, which is the disjoint union of the arcs
\mathbf{e}_{k,j,\alpha,\gamma}
,
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Therefore, whenever
k<\infty
,
\mathbf{T}_{k}
is a maximal tree in
\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
. In the case
k=\infty
, it is worth noting that
p^{-1}(y_{0})\subseteq\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
. Indeed, if
\alpha\in p^{-1}(y_{0})
, find a
j
such that the reduced word
w_{\alpha}
does not end in a letter from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. Then
\alpha
is an arc-endpoint of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
and therefore a free endpoint of
\mathbf{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. Although,
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
is not a tree in the usual sense it will serve as a kind of analogue of a “maximal tree” in
\widetilde{Y}
.

Lemma 5.32
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varrho_{k}(\mathbf{T}_{\infty})=\mathbf{T}_{k}
and
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\mathbf{T}_{k+1})=\mathbf{T}_{k}
.

Proof.

Fix a subspace
\mathbf{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
of
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
. Write
\varrho_{k}(\alpha)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\gamma_{k}
for
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
and
\gamma_{k}\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
. As previously established,
\varrho_{k}
maps
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically onto
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
. In particular
\varrho_{k}
maps the arcs in
\overline{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
to the arcs
\overline{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}\backslash\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
to
\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
. Thus
\varrho_{k}(\mathbf{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha})\subseteq\mathbf{T}_{k}
. The inclusion
\varrho_{k}(\mathbf{T}_{\infty})=\mathbf{T}_{k}
follows for all
k
. It follows immediately that
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\mathbf{T}_{k+1})=\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\varrho_{k+1}(\mathbf{T}_{\infty}))=\varrho_{k}(\mathbf{T}_{\infty})=\mathbf{T}_{k}
.
∎

Lemma 5.33
.

\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
is a uniquely arcwise connected and locally path-connected subspace of
\widetilde{Y}
.

Proof.

Consider distinct points
x,y\in\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
. Find an arc
\ell:I\to\widetilde{Y}
from
x
to
y
. For each
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, the set
W_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash(\overline{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\backslash\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{k,j,\alpha}})
is open in
\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. Since
\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is open in
\widetilde{Y}
,
W_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is open in
\widetilde{Y}
. We will define another path
\eta
that is path-homotopic to
\ell
. If
\ell
already has image in
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
, we take
\eta=\ell
. Otherwise, fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
and let
(a,b)
be a component of
\ell^{-1}(W_{\infty,j,\alpha})
. If
\ell|_{[a,b]}:I\to\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is a loop, then we define
\eta|_{[a,b]}
to be the constant path at
\ell(a)=\ell(b)
. If
\ell
is not a loop, then
\ell(a)
and
\ell(b)
are endpoints in the tree
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. Choose a path
\eta|_{[a,b]}:I\to\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
from
\ell(a)
to
\ell(b)
that parameterizes the unique arc between these points. By construction, we have
\operatorname{Im}(\ell)\subseteq\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
,
\ell(0)=x
, and
\ell(1)=y
so it suffices to verify continuity. Note that we obtain the projection
\varrho_{k}\circ\eta:I\to\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
from
\varrho_{k}\circ\ell:I\to\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
by either making subloops of
\varrho_{k}\circ\ell
constant and possibly modifying finitely many other subpaths. Hence,
\varrho_{k}\circ\eta:I\to\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}
is continuous for each
k
. By Corollary
2.19
,
\eta:I\to\widetilde{Y}
is continuous.

For later in the proof we go ahead and construct a homotopy
H:I^{2}\to\widetilde{Y}
from
\ell
to
\eta
. In particular, if
(a,b)
is a component of
\ell^{-1}(W_{\infty,j,\alpha})
, we define
H
so that
H([a,b]\times I)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. The only part of the construction of
H
that cannot be done arbitrarily is the following: let
U
be a contractible neighborhood of
\ell(a)
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
with contraction
c:U\times I\to U
. If
\ell|_{[a,b]}
is a loop in
U
based at
\ell(a)
, we define
H(s,t)=c(\ell(s),t)
. The argument for the continuity of
H
is the same as it was for
\eta
.

Lemma
5.32
gives
\widetilde{r}_{k+1,k}(\mathbf{T}_{k+1})\subseteq\mathbf{T}_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore, we may form the sub-inverse limit
\varprojlim_{k}\mathbf{T}_{k}
of trees, which is a subspace of
\widehat{Y}
. It is well-known that every path component of an inverse limit of trees is uniquely arcwise connected. Since
\varrho_{k}(\mathbf{T}_{\infty})\subseteq\mathbf{T}_{k}
for all
k
, we have
\phi_{Y}(\mathbf{T}_{\infty})\subseteq\varprojlim_{k}\mathbf{T}_{k}
. Since
\phi_{Y}
continuously injects
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
into a uniquely arcwise connected space, we conclude that
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
is uniquely arcwise connected.

Finally, we check that
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
is locally path connected. Local path connectivity is clear at all points in
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}\backslash p^{-1}(y_{0})
. Let
\alpha\in p^{-1}(y_{0})
and consider a basic neighborhood
N(\alpha,U)\cap\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
of
\alpha
in the subspace topology where
U
is a neighborhood of
y_{0}
in
Y
. We may assume that there is a
J\in\mathbb{N}
such that
Y_{j}\subseteq U
for all
j>J
and
Y_{j}\cap U
is an open arc in
Y_{j}\backslash X_{j}
when
j\leq J
. We will show that
N(\alpha,U)\cap\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
is path connected.

Choose
\alpha\beta\in N(\alpha,U)\cap\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
for
\beta\in\widetilde{U}
(
U
has basepoint
y_{0}
). Note that there exists
j_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(U,y_{0})\cap\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j_{0}}
, and possibly trivial
\delta\in\widetilde{Y}_{j_{0}}\cap p_{j_{0}}^{-1}(U\cap Y_{j_{0}})
such that
\beta=\gamma\delta
. We use
\delta
to denote a path
(I,0)\to(U\cap Y_{j_{0}},y_{0})
representing the homotopy class
\delta
and similarly, we use
\gamma
to denote a reduced loop representing
\gamma
. In the case that
j_{0}\leq J
, we choose
\gamma
to be a parameterization of the arc from
y_{0}
to
\delta(1)\in U\cap Y_{j_{0}}
. Additionally, note that
w_{\gamma}
only has letters from
\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
,
j>J
. Let
\ell_{1}:I\to\widetilde{Y}
be the standard lift of
\gamma
starting at
\alpha
and let
\ell_{2}:I\to\widetilde{Y}
be the standard lift of
\delta
starting at
\alpha\gamma
. Then
\ell_{1}
is a path in
N(\alpha,U)
from
\alpha
to
\alpha\gamma
and
\ell_{2}
is a path in
N(\alpha,U)
from
\alpha\gamma
to
\alpha\beta
. Moreover, the image of
\ell_{1}
only meets
p^{-1}(Y_{j})
for
j>J
. Using the construction in the second paragraph, find paths
\eta_{i}:I\to\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
\eta_{i}
is path-homotopic to
\ell_{i}
in
\widetilde{Y}
. In particular, let
H_{i}:I\times I\to\widetilde{Y}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
be the path-homotopy from
\ell_{i}
to
\eta_{i}
constructed in the third paragraph.

Because we chose
\gamma
to be reduced, we have
\operatorname{Im}(p\circ H_{1})\subseteq U
. Unique path-lifting ensures that
H_{1}
has image in
N(\alpha,U)
and thus
\eta_{1}
has image in
N(\alpha,U)\cap\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
. If
j_{0}\leq J
, then
\delta
has image in
N(\alpha,U)\cap\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
. In this case,
\eta_{2}=\ell_{2}
and
\widetilde{H}_{2}
is the constant homotopy, which makes it clear that
\eta_{2}
has image in
N(\alpha,U)\cap\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
. If
j_{0}>J
, then
\operatorname{Im}(p\circ H_{2})\subseteq U
and thus
H_{2}
has image in
N(\alpha,U)
. Thus
\eta_{2}
also has image in
N(\alpha,U)\cap\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
. Since
\eta_{1}\cdot\eta_{2}
is a path from
\alpha
to
\alpha\beta
in
N(\alpha,U)\cap\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
, we conclude that
N(\alpha,U)\cap\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
is path connected.
∎

Definition 5.34
.

A
topological
\mathbb{R}
-tree
is a topological space, which is metrizable, uniquely arc-wise connected and locally arcwise-connected.

It is known that every topological
\mathbb{R}
-tree may be equipped with an
\mathbb{R}
-tree metric
[
13
]
and that
\mathbb{R}
-trees are contractible
[
14
]

Corollary 5.35
.

If
X_{j}
is locally finite for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
is contractible.

Proof.

As noted in Remark
3.3
, if each
X_{j}
is locally finite, then
\widetilde{Y}
is metrizable. Thus
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
is metrizable. By Lemma
5.33
,
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
meets the other conditions of being a topological
\mathbb{R}
-tree.
∎

Definition 5.36
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
with
j\leq k
, and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
. Identify
\widetilde{f}=\widetilde{f}_{\infty}
in the case
k=\infty
. Define

(1)

\mathbf{E}_{k,j,\alpha}=\widetilde{f}_{k}(\mathbf{T}_{k,j,\infty})
whenever
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq j\leq k
,

(2)

and
\mathbf{E}_{k}=\widetilde{f}_{k}(\mathbf{T}_{k})

as subspaces of
Z_{k}
(
Z
when
k=\infty
).

Remark 5.37
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
we have
\widetilde{f}_{k}^{-1}(\mathbf{E}_{k})=\mathbf{T}_{k}
. Indeed, if
x\in\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\backslash\mathbf{T}_{k}
, then
x\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{k,j,\alpha}\backslash\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha}
for some
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
. This would give
\widetilde{f}(x)\in\mathcal{C}_{k,j,\alpha}\backslash\{c_{k,j,\alpha}\}
, where
\mathcal{C}_{k,j,\alpha}\backslash\{c_{k,j,\alpha}\}
is clearly disjoint from
\mathbf{E}_{k}
. The same reasoning gives
\widetilde{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{E}_{\infty})=\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
.

When
k<\infty
,
\mathbf{E}_{k}
is a tree such that
Z_{k}
consists of
\mathbf{E}_{k}
with the space
\mathcal{C}_{k,j,\alpha}
attached at
c_{k,j,\alpha}\in\mathbf{E}_{k}
.

Lemma 5.38
.

\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
is a uniquely arcwise connected and locally path-connected subspace of
Z
such that

Z=\mathbf{E}_{\infty}\cup\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}.

Moreover, for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, we have

Z=(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha},c_{\infty,j,\alpha})\vee(\overline{Z\backslash\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}},c_{\infty,j,\alpha}).

Proof.

For the moment, fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
. Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\widetilde{f}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
is closed in
\widetilde{Y}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is closed in
Z
. Additionally, recall from the start of the proof of Lemma
5.33
that
W_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash(\overline{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}\backslash\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{k,j,\alpha}})
is open in
\widetilde{Y}
. Since
W_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\widetilde{f}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash\{c_{\infty,j,\alpha}\})
, the set
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash\{c_{\infty,j,\alpha}\}
is open in
Z
. This is enough to give the wedge-sum factorization

Z=(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha},c_{\infty,j,\alpha})\vee(\overline{Z\backslash\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}},c_{\infty,j,\alpha}).

Since the above paragraph holds for all pairs
(j,\alpha)
and

\mathbf{E}_{\infty}=Z\backslash\left(\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash\{c_{\infty,j,\alpha}\}\right),

it follows that
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
is closed in
Z
. Recall from Remark
5.37
that
\widetilde{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{E}_{\infty})=\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
and so the restricted map
\widetilde{f}|_{\mathbf{T}_{\infty}}:\mathbf{T}_{\infty}\to\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
is a quotient map. Since
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
is path connected and locally path connected by Lemma
5.33
, so is its quotient space
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
. Additionally, Lemma
5.33
gives that
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
is uniquely arcwise connected. Every fiber of
\widetilde{f}|_{\mathbf{T}_{\infty}}:\mathbf{T}_{\infty}\to\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
is either a point or a closed sub-tree of the form
\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. It follows easily that every point in
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
separates
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
into two disjoint, open components. Therefore,
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
is uniquely arcwise connected.
∎

The second statement of Lemma
5.38
implies that for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, there is a retraction
\mu_{j,\alpha}:Z\to\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
, that collapses
Z\backslash\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
to
c_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. Additionally, there is a retraction
\mu:Z\to\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
, which collapses each
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
to
c_{\infty,j,\alpha}
.

Corollary 5.39
.

The spaces
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
are retracts of
Z
.

Corollary 5.40
.

If
D
is a Peano continuum in
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
or
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
, then
D
is a dendrite.

Theorem 5.41
.

The restricted maps
\widetilde{f}|_{\mathbf{T}_{\infty}}:\mathbf{T}_{\infty}\to\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
and
\widetilde{g}|_{\mathbf{T}_{\infty}}:\mathbf{E}_{\infty}\to\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
are based homotopy inverses.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
and
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
contain the basepoints of
\widetilde{Y}
and
Z
respectively, the corresponding restrictions of
\widetilde{H}
and
\widetilde{G}
will verify the desired homotopy equivalence if we show that
\widetilde{g}(\mathbf{E}_{\infty})\subseteq\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
,
\widetilde{H}(\mathbf{T}_{\infty}\times I)\subseteq\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
, and
\widetilde{G}(\mathbf{E}_{\infty}\times I)\subseteq\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
. Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
. Recall that each of
\widetilde{g}
,
\widetilde{H}
, and
\widetilde{G}
are determined by their restrictions
\widetilde{g}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
,
\widetilde{H}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
, and
\widetilde{G}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. Therefore, it suffices to check that
\widetilde{g}_{\infty,j,\alpha}(\mathbf{E}_{\infty,j,\alpha})\subseteq\mathbf{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
,
\widetilde{H}_{\infty,j,\alpha}(\mathbf{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I)\subseteq\mathbf{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
, and
\widetilde{G}_{\infty,j,\alpha}(\mathbf{E}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I)\subseteq\mathbf{E}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. In all cases, we use the fact that
\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}(\mathbf{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha})=\beta_{\alpha}\mathbf{T}_{j}
and
\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}(\mathbf{E}_{\infty,j,\alpha})=E_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}
.

From the left square in Proposition
5.30
, we have
g_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}^{-1}\circ g_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}\circ\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. Recall from Section
3.3
that we have
g_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}(E_{j,\beta_{\alpha}})\subseteq\beta_{\alpha}\mathbf{T}_{j}
. If follows that

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{\infty,j,\alpha}(\mathbf{E}_{\infty,j,\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}^{-1}\circ g_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}\circ\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}(\mathbf{E}_{\infty,j,\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}^{-1}\circ g_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}(E_{j,\beta_{\alpha}})

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}^{-1}(\beta_{\alpha}\mathbf{T}_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}

The same argument using the square in Lemma
5.28
for
\widetilde{H}
and the right square in Proposition
5.30
for
\widetilde{G}
gives the other inclusions.
∎

Combining the previous theorem with Corollary
5.35
, we have the following.

Corollary 5.42
.

If
X_{j}
is locally finite for every
j
, then
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
is contractible.

Even when
X_{j}
is not locally finite, every Peano continuum in
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
is a dendrite and therefore contractible. Nevertheless, the author anticipates that
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
is contractible even when
X_{j}
is not locally finite. However, this requires a characterization of contractible, uniquely arcwise connected, and locally arcwise connected spaces, which apparently does not exist in the current literature.

5.5
Shrinking adjunction spaces in
Z

Definition 5.43
.

Let
S
be
\{1,2,\dots M\}
or
\mathbb{N}
. Let
D
be a path-connected space,
\{d_{i}\}_{i\in S}
be a sequence (of not necessarily distinct points) in
D
and
\{(A_{i},a_{i})\}_{i\in S}
be a corresponding sequence of based, connected CW-complexes. The
shrinking adjunction space with core
D
and attachment spaces
\{(A_{i},a_{i})\}_{i\in S}
is the space
\mathbf{X}=X\sqcup\coprod_{i\in S}A_{i}/\mathord{\sim}
where
d_{i}\sim a_{i}
for each
i\in S
. We give
\mathbf{X}
the following topology: a set
U\subseteq\mathbf{X}
is open if and only if

(1)

D\cap U
is open in
D
,

(2)

A_{i}\cap U
is open in
A_{i}
for all
i\in S
,

(3)

whenever
S=\mathbb{N}
and
i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}<\cdots
such that
\{d_{i_{m}}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to a point
d\in D\cap U
, we have
A_{i_{m}}\subseteq U
for all but finitely many
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Note that the case in Definition
5.43
where
S
is finite is simply to allow for an ordinary finite adjunction space to be considered as a degenerate case of a shrinking adjunction space. Note that
D
and all attachment spaces
A_{i}
are retracts of
\mathbf{X}
as defined above. Let
u_{i}:\mathbf{X}\to A_{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
be the retraction that collapses
D
and
A_{j}
for
j\neq i
to
a_{i}
. The following is one of the main results of
[
5
]
.

Theorem 5.44
.

Let
n\geq 2
and
\mathbf{X}
be a shrinking adjunction space as described in Definition
5.43
where
D
is a Peano continuum with basepoint
d_{0}
. Then canonical homomorphism
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{X}}:\pi_{n}(\mathbf{X},d_{0})\to\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{n}(A_{i},a_{i})
,
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{X}}([\ell])=([u_{i}\circ\ell])
is a split epimorphism. Moreover, if
D
is a dendrite and
A_{i}
is an
(n-1)
-connected CW-complex for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{X}}
is an isomorphism.

In the next lemma we identify the relevant shrinking adjunction spaces within
Z
.

Lemma 5.45
.

Let
D\subseteq\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
be a dendrite and
\{(j_{i},\alpha_{i})\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of distinct pairs where
j_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j_{i}}
, and
c_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}}\in D
. Then
\{j_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\to\infty
and
\mathcal{P}=D\cup\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}}
is a shrinking adjunction space with core
D
and attachment spaces
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}}
.

Proof.

To simplify our notation, we write
A_{i}
for
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}}
.

First, we prove that
\{j_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\to\infty
. If
\{j_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\nrightarrow\infty
, then there exists
J\in\mathbb{N}
and
i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}<\cdots
such that
j_{i_{m}}=J
and that
\alpha_{i_{m}}\neq\alpha_{i_{m^{\prime}}}
whenever
m\neq m^{\prime}
. Since
\{c_{\infty,J,\alpha_{i_{m}}}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence in the compact set
D
, we may replace
\{i_{m}\}
by a subsequence so that
\{c_{\infty,J,\alpha_{i_{m}}}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\to z
for some
z\in D
. Since all pairs
(J,\alpha_{i_{m}})
are distinct and
Z\backslash\widetilde{f}(p^{-1}(y_{0}))
is the disjoint union of the open sets
\widetilde{f}(\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
, we must have
z=\widetilde{f}(\alpha)
for some
\alpha\in p^{-1}(y_{0})
. Since
\widetilde{g}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,J,\alpha_{i_{m}}})\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,J,\alpha_{i_{m}}}
, we have
a_{m}=\widetilde{g}(c_{\infty,J,\alpha_{i_{m}}})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,J,\alpha_{i_{m}}}
where
\{a_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\to\widetilde{g}(z)=\alpha
. Thus
\{p(a_{m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\to y_{0}
in
Y
where
p(a_{m})\in X_{J}
for all
m
. This is a contradiction since
X_{J}
is closed in
Y
and
y_{0}\notin X_{J}
.

With
\{j_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\to\infty
established, note that the subspace
\mathcal{P}
of
Z
has the underlying set of the desired shrinking adjunction space. Since
D
and all
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
are retracts of
Z
, Conditions (1) and (2) in Definition
5.43
hold; it suffices to check Condition (3). Recall that the attachment points in
D
are
d_{i_{m}}=c_{\infty,j_{i_{m}},\alpha_{i_{m}}}
. Suppose
U
is an open set in
Z
and that
i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}<\cdots
is such that
\{d_{i_{m}}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\to d
for
d\in U
. Suppose, to obtain a contradiction, that there exist
m_{1}<m_{2}<m_{3}<\cdots
such that
A_{i_{m_{r}}}
is not contained in
U
. By replacing
\{d_{i_{m}}\}
with the corresponding subsequence, we may assume that
A_{i_{m}}\nsubseteq U
for all
m
. Then there exists
a_{m}\in A_{i_{m}}\backslash U
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
\{d_{i_{m}}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\to d
,
d_{i_{m}}\in U
for all but finitely many
m
. Thus we may assume
a_{m}\neq d_{i_{m}}
, i.e.
a_{m}\in A_{i_{m}}\backslash\{d_{i_{m}}\}
.

Using our characterization of the structure of
Z
again, we must have
d\in D\cap\widetilde{f}(p^{-1}(x_{0}))
. Since
\widetilde{f}
is bijective on
p^{-1}(x_{0})
(with inverse
\widetilde{g}
), there is a unique
\beta=\widetilde{g}(d)\in p^{-1}(x_{0})\subseteq\widetilde{Y}
with
\widetilde{f}(\beta)=d
. Choose
\beta_{m}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j_{i_{m}},\alpha_{i_{m}}}
with
\widetilde{f}(\beta_{m})=a_{m}
. For each
m
, we have
\beta_{m}=\alpha_{i_{m}}\gamma_{m}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j_{i_{m}},\alpha_{i_{m}}}
for
\gamma_{m}\in\widetilde{Y}_{j_{i_{m}}}
.

Since
\{d_{i_{m}}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}=\{c_{\infty,j_{i_{m}},\alpha_{i_{m}}}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\to d
in
Z
, we have
\{\widetilde{g}(d_{i_{m}})\}\to\beta
in
\widetilde{Y}
. The definition of
\widetilde{g}
ensures that
\widetilde{g}(d_{i_{m}})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j_{i_{m}},\alpha_{i_{m}}}
. Thus
\widetilde{g}(d_{i_{m}})=\alpha_{i_{m}}\delta_{m}
for
\delta_{m}\in\widetilde{Y}_{j_{i_{m}}}
. In summary,
\{\alpha_{i_{m}}\delta_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\to\beta
in
\widetilde{Y}
for
\delta_{m}\in\widetilde{Y}_{j_{i_{m}}}
.

Since
\widetilde{f}(\beta)=d\in U
,
\widetilde{f}^{-1}(U)
is an open neighborhood of
\beta
in
\widetilde{Y}
. Find a basic open neighborhood
N(\beta,V)\subseteq\widetilde{f}^{-1}(U)
where
V
is an open neighborhood of
y_{0}
in
Y
. We may assume that there is a
J
such that
Y_{j}\subseteq V
for all
j\geq J
. Since
\{j_{i_{m}}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\to\infty
, we may find
M
such that
j_{i_{m}}\geq J
for all
m\geq M
. Additionally, since
\{\alpha_{i_{m}}\delta_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\to\beta
, we may choose
M
large enough so that
\alpha_{i_{m}}\delta_{m}\in N(\beta,V)
for all
m\geq M
. Fix
m\geq M
. A path representing
\delta_{m}
has image in
Y_{j_{i_{m}}}\subseteq V
and thus
\alpha_{i_{m}}\in N(\beta,V)
. It follows that
N(\beta,V)=N(\alpha_{i_{m}},V)
. We have
\gamma_{i_{m}}\in\widetilde{Y}_{j_{i_{m}}}
and
j_{i_{m}}\geq J
. Thus
\beta_{m}=\alpha_{i_{m}}\gamma_{i_{m}}\in N(\alpha_{i_{m}},V)=N(\beta,V)\subseteq\widetilde{f}^{-1}(U)
. This gives
a_{m}=\widetilde{f}(\beta_{m})\in U
; a contradiction.
∎

We refer to a subspace of
Z
of the form
\mathcal{P}=D\cup\bigcup_{i}\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}}
as described in Lemma
5.45
as a
D
-subcomplex of
Z
.
Note that every
D
-subcomplex of
Z
is a retract of
Z
. Indeed, since
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
is uniquely arcwise connected and locally arcwise connected, there is a canonical retraction
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}\to D
and this can easily be extended to a retract of
Z
using the second statement of Lemma
5.38
.

Corollary 5.46
.

Every Peano continuum in
Z
containing
z_{0}
is a subset of a
D
-subcomplex of
Z
for some dendrite
D\subseteq\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Let
P\subseteq Z
be a Peano continuum containing
z_{0}
. Then
D=P\cap\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
is a dendrite. Since the sets
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash\{c_{\infty,j,\alpha}\}
are open and disjoint in
Z
(ranging over all pairs
(j,\alpha)
),
P
can meet
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash\{c_{\infty,j,\alpha}\}
for at most countably many pairs
(j,\alpha)
. Otherwise, the separability of
P
would be violated. Let
(j_{i},\alpha_{i})
be an enumeration of the pairs
(j,\alpha)
for which
P
meets
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash\{c_{\infty,j,\alpha}\}
. If the sequence
\{(j_{i},\alpha_{i})\}_{i}
is finite, we define
P
to be the finite union
\mathcal{P}=D\cup\bigcup_{i}\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
(this is trivially a shrinking adjunction space) and it is clear that
P\subseteq\mathcal{P}
. We now assume that
\{(j_{i},\alpha_{i})\}
is indexed by
\mathbb{N}
. Since
P
is path connected, we must have
c_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}}\in D
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. By Lemma
5.45
,
\mathcal{P}=D\cup\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}}
is a
D
-subcomplex of
Z
. Clearly,
P\subseteq\mathcal{P}
.
∎

Let
\mathfrak{DS}
be the set of
D
-subcomplexes
\mathcal{P}
in
Z
such that
z_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
. Subset inclusion defines a partial ordering of
\mathfrak{DS}
. Whenever
\mathcal{P}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}
in
\mathfrak{DS}
,
\mathcal{P}_{1}
is a retract of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
and so we have a canonical injective homomorphism
\varphi_{\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}}:\pi_{n}(\mathcal{P}_{1},z_{0})\to\pi_{n}(\mathcal{P}_{2},z_{0})
. These maps form a directed system of injective homomorphism for which we have the direct limit
\varinjlim_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{DS}}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{P},z_{0})
. Moreover, since each
\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{DS}
is a retract of
Z
, the homomorphism
\varphi_{\mathcal{P}}:\pi_{n}(\mathcal{P},z_{0})\to\pi_{n}(Z,z_{0})
induced by inclusion
\mathcal{P}\to Z
is injective.

Theorem 5.47
.

For all
n\geq 2
, inclusion maps
\mathcal{P}\to Z
,
\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{DS}
induce a canonical isomorphism
\varphi:\varinjlim_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathfrak{DS}}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{P},z_{0})\to\pi_{n}(Z,z_{0})
.

Proof.

Injectivity of each
\varphi_{\mathcal{P}}
ensures that
\varphi
is injective. Surjectivity is a direct consequence of Corollary
5.46
.
∎

Unfortunately, the isomorphism in Theorem
5.47
is impractical for understanding
\pi_{n}(Z)
in terms of the homotopy groups of the spaces
X_{j}
. We provide another approach in the next section.

6
Main Results

6.1
The homomorphism
\Psi
and its image

The previous section implies that every map
\ell:I^{n}\to Z
will have image in some
D
-subcomplex of
Z
. Hence
\ell
can meet countably many of the space
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash\{c_{\infty,j,\alpha}\}
. Here, we show that an even stronger statement holds: we can deform
\ell
so that for any fixed
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\ell
will only meet
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash\{c_{\infty,j,\alpha}\}
for finitely many pairs
(j,\alpha)
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Every map
\ell:(I^{n},\partial I^{n})\to(Z,z_{0})
is homotopic rel.
\partial I^{n}
to a map
\ell^{\prime\prime}:(I^{n},\partial I^{n})\to(Z,z_{0})
such that for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
, the set of connected components of
(\ell^{\prime\prime})^{-1}(\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash\{c_{\infty,j,\alpha}\})
is finite.

Proof.

Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\ell:(I^{n},\partial I^{n})\to(Z,z_{0})
be a map. Since
\widetilde{f}\circ\widetilde{g}\simeq id_{Z}
, we have
\ell^{\prime}=\widetilde{f}\circ\widetilde{g}\circ\ell\simeq\ell
. Let
\widetilde{\kappa}=\widetilde{g}\circ\ell
and
\kappa=p\circ\widetilde{\kappa}:(I^{n},\partial I^{n})\to(Y,y_{0})
.

\textstyle{(I^{n},\partial I^{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\ell^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}}
\textstyle{Z}

It suffices to verify the lemma for
\ell^{\prime}
. We will use
\ell^{\prime}
to define a new map
\ell^{\prime\prime}:(I^{n},\partial I^{n})\to(Z,z_{0})
. First, we define
\ell^{\prime\prime}
to agree with
\ell^{\prime}
on the closed set
(\ell^{\prime})^{-1}(\mathbf{E}_{\infty})
.

Let
U
be a contractible neighborhood of
c_{j}
in
C_{j}
and let
U_{j,\alpha}=\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}^{-1}(U)
be the corresponding neighborhood of
c_{\infty,j,\alpha}
in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. Let
K_{j,\alpha}:U_{j,\alpha}\times I\to U_{j,\alpha}
be a based contraction, i.e. a based homotopy from the identity of
U_{j,\alpha}
to the constant map at
c_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. Fixing
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, consider the open set
V_{\alpha}=(\ell^{\prime})^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash c_{\infty,j,\alpha})
in
(0,1)^{n}
. If
W
is a connected component of
V_{\alpha}
, then
\ell^{\prime}(\partial W)=c_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. Now, if
\ell^{\prime}(W)\subseteq U_{j,\alpha}
, we define
\ell^{\prime\prime}
to be constant on
\overline{W}
, that is,
\ell^{\prime\prime}(\overline{W})=c_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. On the other hand if
\ell^{\prime}(W)\nsubseteq U_{j,\alpha}
, we define
\ell^{\prime\prime}
to agree with
\ell^{\prime}
on
W
. Define a homotopy
L:I^{n}\times I\to Z
as follows:
L
is the constant homotopy on
(\ell^{\prime})^{-1}(\mathbf{E}_{\infty})
and on any component
W
of
V_{\alpha}
where
\ell^{\prime}(W)\nsubseteq U_{j,\alpha}
. If
W
is a component of
V_{\alpha}
where
\ell^{\prime}(W)\subseteq U_{j,\alpha}
, we define
L
so that
L|_{W\times I}(w,t)=K_{j,\alpha}(\ell^{\prime}(w),t)
for
w\in W
,
t\in I
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, it is straightforward to check that the projection
\sigma_{k}\circ L:I^{n}\times I\to Z_{k}
is continuous. By Corollary
5.24
,
L:I^{n}\times I\to\widehat{Z}
is continuous. Thus
L
is a homotopy from
\ell^{\prime}
to
\ell^{\prime\prime}
.

To check that
\ell^{\prime\prime}
has the desired property, suppose there is a
j\in\mathbb{N}
, distinct
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\dots\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, and points
x_{i}\in I^{n}
such that
\ell^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})\in\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha_{i}}\backslash\{c_{\infty,j,\alpha_{i}}\}
. By our construction of
\ell^{\prime\prime}
, we must have
\ell^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})=\ell^{\prime}(x_{i})=\widetilde{f}\circ\widetilde{\kappa}(x_{i})\in\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha_{i}}
. Thus
\widetilde{\kappa}(x_{i})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha_{i}}
in
\widetilde{Y}
. Replacing
\{x_{i}\}
with a subsequence, we may assume
\{x_{i}\}\to x
in
I^{n}
. The subsets
\widetilde{f}(\mathcal{U}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
of
Z
are all disjoint and open in
Z
and the same is true of the subsets
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash\{c_{\infty,j,\alpha}\}
. This observation with the fact that the
\alpha_{i}
are all distinct, gives
\widetilde{f}\circ\widetilde{\kappa}(x)\in\widetilde{f}(p^{-1}(y_{0}))
. Since
\widetilde{\kappa}(x)\in p^{-1}(y_{0})
, we have
\kappa(x)=y_{0}\in Y
. In summary, we have
\kappa(x_{i})\to y_{0}
in
Y
where
x_{i}\in X_{j}
. However, this is a contradiction since
j
is fixed,
X_{j}
is closed in
Y
, and
y_{0}\notin X_{j}
. We conclude that
(\ell^{\prime\prime})^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\backslash\{c_{\infty,j,\alpha}\})
is non-empty for finitely many
\alpha
and has finitely many components when it is non-empty.
∎

Recall that
\mu_{j,\alpha}:Z\to\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
denotes the canonical retraction.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
n\geq 2
. There is a canonical group homomorphism

\Psi:\pi_{n}(Z,z_{0})\to\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha})

given by
\Psi([\ell])=([\mu_{j,\alpha}\circ\ell])
.

Proof.

It is clear that
\Psi
well-defined with codomain
\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\prod_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
. Lemma
6.1
ensures that
\Psi
has image in the subgroup
\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
.
∎

Next we characterize the image of
\Psi
using the topology on
\pi_{1}(Y)
.

Theorem 6.3
.

If
[\ell_{j,\alpha}]\in\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, then
([\ell_{j,\alpha}])\in\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
is in the image of
\Psi
if and only if the closure of
\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}\mid[\ell_{j,\alpha}]\neq 0\}
in
\pi_{1}(Y)
is compact.

Proof.

Suppose
t:(I^{n},\partial I^{n})\to(Z,z_{0})
and let
t_{j,\alpha}=\mu_{j,\alpha}\circ t
. Using the based homotopy inverses
\widetilde{f}
and
\widetilde{g}
, we may assume that
t=\widetilde{g}\circ\widetilde{\kappa}
for
\widetilde{\kappa}:(I^{n},\partial I^{n})\to(\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{y}_{0})
. Note that
\widetilde{f}(\alpha)
lies on the unique arc in
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
from
z_{0}
to
c_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. Therefore, if
[t_{j,\alpha}]\neq 0
, we must have
\widetilde{f}(\alpha)\in\operatorname{Im}(t)
. Since
\widetilde{f}
is bijective on
p^{-1}(y_{0})
(with inverse
\widetilde{g}
) we must have
\alpha\in\operatorname{Im}\left(\widetilde{\kappa}\right)
. Thus
\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}\mid[t_{j,\alpha}]\neq 1\}\subseteq\operatorname{Im}\left(\widetilde{\kappa}\right)\cap p^{-1}(y_{0})=\operatorname{Im}(\widetilde{\kappa})\cap\pi_{1}(Y)
. Since
\operatorname{Im}(\widetilde{\kappa})
is compact and
\pi_{1}(Y)
is closed in
\widetilde{Y}
,
\operatorname{Im}\left(\widetilde{\kappa}\right)\cap\pi_{1}(Y)
is compact as a subspace of
\pi_{1}(Y)
. It follows that the closure of
\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}\mid[t_{j,\alpha}]\neq 0\}
in
\pi_{1}(Y)
is contained in
\operatorname{Im}\left(\widetilde{\kappa}\right)\cap\pi_{1}(Y)
and therefore compact.

For the converse, suppose
(a_{j,\alpha})
is an element of the codomain such that the closure of
A=\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}\mid a_{j,\alpha}\neq 0\}
in
\pi_{1}(Y)
is compact. If
A
is finite, then standard methods may be used to construct a map
t:(I^{n},\partial I^{n})\to(Z,z_{0})
such that
\Psi([t])=(a_{j,\alpha})
. From now on, we assume that
A
is infinite with compact closure. Let
B=A\cup\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\{\alpha\tau_{j}\in\widetilde{Y}\mid a_{j,\alpha}\neq 1\}
. We have extended
A
to
B
so that
c_{\infty,j,\alpha}\in\widetilde{f}(B)
whenever
a_{j,\alpha}\neq 0
.

First, we claim that
\overline{B}
is compact using a the following fact: for a sequence
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\overline{A}
with
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j_{i}}
, we have
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\to\alpha
in
\widetilde{Y}
(and thus
\overline{A}
) if and only if
\{\alpha_{i}\tau_{j_{i}}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\to\alpha
. The proof of this argument follows the same reasoning used in the proof of Lemma
5.45
so we omit it. To prove compactness, suppose
\mathscr{U}^{\prime}
is an open cover of
\overline{B}
and let
\mathscr{U}\subseteq\mathscr{U}^{\prime}
be a finite subset that covers
\overline{A}
. It is enough to show that there are only finitely many points of the form
\alpha\tau_{j}
not in
\bigcup\mathscr{U}
. To obtain a contradiction, suppose
\alpha_{i}\tau_{j_{i}}\notin\bigcup\mathscr{U}
for an infinite sequence
\{(j_{i},\alpha_{i})\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of distinct pairs. However, since
\overline{A}
is compact,
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
has a convergent subsequence
\{\alpha_{i_{m}}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\to\alpha
where
\alpha\in\overline{A}
. Thus
\alpha_{i_{m}}\tau_{j_{i_{m}}}\to\alpha
in
\widetilde{Y}
. However,
\alpha\in\bigcup\mathscr{U}
and so
\alpha_{i_{m}}\tau_{j_{i_{m}}}\in\bigcup\mathscr{U}
for sufficiently large
m
; a contradiction.

With the compactness of
\overline{B}
established, let
\mathbf{D}
be the union of all arcs in
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
from
\widetilde{y}_{0}
the points of
\overline{B}
. Clearly,
\mathbf{D}
is uniquely arcwise connected. It is straightforward to show that
\mathbf{D}
is compact using the compactness of
\overline{B}
. It follows that
\mathbf{D}
and
D=\widetilde{f}(\mathbf{D})
are dendrites. By our construction of
B
, we have
c_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}}\in D
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, by Lemma
5.45
,
\mathcal{P}=D\cup\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{C}_{\alpha,j_{i},\alpha_{i}}
is a
D
-subcomplex of
Z
.

Let
\iota:\mathcal{P}\to Z
be the inclusion and
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{P}}:\pi_{n}(\mathcal{P},z_{0})\to\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{n}(A_{i})
be the canonical homomorphism from Theorem
5.44
induced by the retractions
\mathcal{P}\to A_{i}
. Let
\Xi:\prod_{i}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}})\to\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
be the inclusion map induced by the projections
\prod_{i}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}})\to\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}})
(and trivial maps in the other coordinates). It is straightforward to check that the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\mathcal{P},z_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Upsilon_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\#}}
\textstyle{\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha})}

Since
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{P}}
is surjective by Theorem
5.44
, we have
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{P}}([\ell])=(a_{j_{i},\alpha_{i}})_{i}
for some
[\ell]\in\pi_{n}(\mathcal{P},z_{0})
. Now
\Psi([\iota\circ\ell])=(a_{j,\alpha})_{j,\alpha}
for
[\iota\circ\ell]\in\pi_{n}(Z)
. This completes the proof that
\Psi
is surjective.
∎

Problem 6.4
.

According to Theorem
5.44
, the homomorphism
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{P}}
always has a section. However, it is not clear to the author if these can be chosen in a coherent way. Does
\Psi:\pi_{n}(Z)\to\operatorname{Im}(\Psi)
always split?

6.2
The injectivity of
\Psi
when each
\widetilde{X}_{j}
is
(n-1)
-connected

In this section, we identify a case where
\Psi
is injective. In particular, we fix
n\geq 2
and suppose that
\pi_{m}(Y_{j})=0
for
2\leq m\leq n-1
or, equivalently, that
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
is
(n-1)
-connected. We will directly use the injectivity result in Theorem
5.44
.

Theorem 6.5
.

If
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
is
(n-1)
-connected for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, then the canonical homomorphism

\Psi:\pi_{n}(Z)\to\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha})

is injective.

Proof.

First, note that since
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
is
(n-1)
-connected and
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\cong\widetilde{Y}_{j}/\beta_{\alpha}T_{j}\simeq\widetilde{Y}_{j}
, it follows that
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is
(n-1)
-connected.

Let
\ell:(I^{n},\partial I^{n})\to(Z,z_{0})
be a map such that
\Psi([\ell])=0
. Since
\operatorname{Im}(\ell)
is a Peano continuum in
Z
, by Corollary
5.46
, we may find a
D
-subcomplex
\mathcal{P}=D\cup\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}}
containing
\operatorname{Im}(\ell)
. In particular, the core is
D=\operatorname{Im}(\ell)\cap\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
and the attachment spaces are
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}}
for a (possibly finite) non-repeating sequence of pairs
(j_{i},\alpha_{i})
. By assumption the projection
\ell_{j,\alpha}=\mu_{j,\alpha}\circ\ell:(I^{n},\partial I^{n})\to(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha},c_{\infty,j,\alpha})
is null-homotopic in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
for all pairs
(j,\alpha)
. In particular, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\ell_{j_{i},\alpha_{i}}
is null-homotopic in
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}}
.

Let
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{P}}:\pi_{n}(\mathcal{P},z_{0})\to\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}},c_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}})
be the canonical homomorphism induced by the retractions
\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}}
. According to Theorem
5.44
,
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{P}}
is an isomorphism. Let
\iota:\mathcal{P}\to Z
be the inclusion map. As in the proof of Theorem
6.3
, let
\Xi
be canonical the inclusion homomorphism so that the following square commutes.

\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\mathcal{P})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Upsilon_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\#}}
\textstyle{\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j_{i},\alpha_{i}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha})}

Viewing
\ell
as a map
I^{n}\to P
with
\iota\circ\ell=\ell
, we have
\Xi\circ\Upsilon_{P}([\ell])=\Psi([\ell])=0
by assumption. Since
\Xi
and
\Upsilon_{P}
are injective,
[\ell]=0
in
\pi_{n}(\mathcal{P})
. Thus
[\ell]=0
in
\pi_{n}(Z)
.
∎

6.3
The homomorphism
\Theta
and a proof of Theorem
1.1

Finally, we put everything together to prove the main result of this paper.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

For each
1\leq j\leq k
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
, there is a canonical retraction
b_{k,j,\alpha}:\widetilde{Y}_{\leq k}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{k,j,\alpha}
. Fixing
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, and letting
\varrho_{k}(\alpha)=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\gamma_{k}
for
\alpha_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{k,j}
, the
\operatorname{lpc}
-coreflection of the inverse limit
\varprojlim_{k\geq j}b_{k,j,\alpha_{k}^{\prime}}
gives a canonical retraction
b_{\infty,j,\alpha}:\widetilde{Y}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. In short,
b_{\infty,j,\alpha}
maps points outside of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
to the “nearest” arc-endpoint of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
.

Recall that we are still identifying
\widetilde{X}_{j}
as a subspace of
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
as a subspace of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
. The quotient maps
\zeta_{j}:Y_{j}\to X_{j}
collapsing the attached arc form the quotient map
\zeta:Y\to X
, which induces the homotopy equivalence
\widetilde{\zeta}:\widetilde{Y}\to\widetilde{X}
. Since
\widetilde{\zeta}
collapses the arcs of each
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
and maps
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically onto its image, we will also write
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
to denote the subspace
\widetilde{\zeta}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
of
\widetilde{X}
. Let
\widetilde{\zeta}_{\infty,j,\alpha}:\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
be the corresponding restriction of
\widetilde{\zeta}
. There is a canonical homeomorphism
\Gamma_{\infty,j,\alpha}:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\to\widetilde{X}_{j}
such that the following square commutes.

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\zeta}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\zeta}_{j}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{X}_{j}}

In the same way, we constructed the maps
b_{\infty,j,\alpha}
, we may construct canonical maps
a_{\infty,j,\alpha}:\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
such that the following square commutes.

\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\zeta}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\zeta}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}}

Note that both of the above squares are diagrams of based maps, Although, we identify them in our notation, the basepoint of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is
\alpha\tau_{j}
if viewed as a subspace of
\widetilde{Y}
and
\widetilde{\zeta}(\alpha)
if viewed as a subspace of
\widetilde{X}
. Putting it all together, we consider the following diagram where all products are indexed over pairs
(j,\alpha)
with
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
. For example,
\prod\pi_{n}(Y_{j})
denotes
\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\prod_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\pi_{n}(Y_{j})
.

\textstyle{\pi_{n}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{\prod\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha})}
\textstyle{\prod\pi_{n}(\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{\#}}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\widetilde{Y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\zeta}_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{p_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\prod\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\prod(f_{\infty,j,\alpha})_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda}
\scriptstyle{\prod(\widetilde{\zeta}_{\infty,j,\alpha})_{\#}}
\textstyle{\prod\pi_{n}(Y_{j})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\prod\zeta_{j\#}}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\widetilde{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{\#}}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{\prod\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma}
\textstyle{\prod\pi_{n}(X_{j})}

In the above diagram,

•

R
is the product of the isomorphisms induced by the retractions
\mathcal{D}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\to\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
that collapse the attached arcs to
c_{\infty,j,\alpha}
.

•

b
is induced by the maps
(b_{\infty,j,\alpha})_{\#}:\pi_{n}(\widetilde{Y})\to\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
,

•

a
is induced by the maps
(a_{\infty,j,\alpha})_{\#}:\pi_{n}(\widetilde{X})\to\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
,

•

\Lambda=\prod(p_{j}\circ\Lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha})_{\#}
,

•

\Gamma=\prod(q_{j}\circ\Gamma_{\infty,j,\alpha})_{\#}
,

•

and
\Theta=\Gamma\circ a\circ q_{\#}^{-1}
.

The bottom left square commutes by the definition of
\widetilde{\zeta}
. Commutativity of the other two bottom squares follows from the two squares given earlier in the proof. Because
\Psi
is induced by the retractions
Z\to\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
, a direct check shows the top square commutes. Although the upper square depends on the choice of the trees
T_{j}
, the lower squares do not. Previous results ensure that all maps except for
\Psi
,
a
, and
b
are isomorphisms.

Recall from Theorem
6.2
that
\Psi
has image in
\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
. It follows from the diagram that
\Theta
has image in
\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}}\pi_{n}(X_{j})
. Finally, recall that there are canonical bijections
\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}\to\pi_{1}(Y)/\pi_{1}(Y_{j})\to\pi_{1}(X)/\pi_{1}(X_{j})
(the first is the restriction of the projection
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(Y)/\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
and the second is induced by
\zeta
). Thus we may canonically identify the indexing sets
\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
and
\pi_{1}(X)/\pi_{1}(X_{j})
. This gives the desired homomorphism
\Theta
as described in the statement of Theorem
1.1
.

Lastly, when
\widetilde{X}_{j}
is
(n-1)
-connected for all
j\in\mathbb{N}

\widetilde{Y}_{j}
is
(n-1)
-connected for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. The homomorphism
\Psi
is injective by Theorem
6.5
. It follows from the diagram that
b
and
a
are injective. We conclude that
\Theta
is injective.
∎

Remark 6.6

(The image of
\Theta
)
.

Combining Theorem
6.3
with the diagram from the proof of Theorem
1.1
, a direct proof gives the following characterization of
Im(\Theta)
: Letting
H_{j}=\pi_{1}(X_{j})
, we denote elements of
\pi_{1}(X)/H_{j}
by
\beta H_{j}
. An element
(\ell_{j,\beta H_{j}})\in\prod_{j}\bigoplus_{\pi_{1}(X)/H_{j}}\pi_{n}(X_{j})
lies in
Im(\Theta)
if and only if the closure of
\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\{\beta\in\pi_{1}(X)\mid\ell_{j,\beta H_{j}}\neq 0\}
in
\pi_{1}(X)
(with the whisker topology) is compact.

With Theorem
1.1
established, we identifying an alternative description of
\pi_{n}(X)
using the
n
-shape homotopy group
\check{\pi}_{n}(X)=\varprojlim_{k}\pi_{n}(X_{\leq k})
.

Corollary 6.7
.

Suppose
\widetilde{X}_{j}
is
(n-1)
connected for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the canonical homomorphism
\Phi:\pi_{n}(X)\to\check{\pi}_{n}(X)
,
\Phi([\ell])=([r_{k}\circ\ell])
to the
n
-th shape homotopy group is injective.

Proof.

The homotopy equivalence
\zeta:Y\to X
induces an isomorphism on
\pi_{n}
and
\check{\pi}_{n}
. Since
\Phi
is natural, it suffices to prove the result for
Y
.

Suppose
0\neq[\ell]\in\pi_{n}(Y)
. Let
\widetilde{\ell}:S^{n}\to\widetilde{Y}
be the lift of
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}=\widetilde{f}\circ\widetilde{\ell}
. Since
\widetilde{f}
and
p
induce isomorphisms on
\pi_{n}
, we have
0\neq[\ell^{\prime}]\in\pi_{n}(Z)
. By Theorem
2.2
, there exists
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
such that
0\neq[\mu_{j,\alpha}\circ\ell^{\prime}]\in\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha})
. Now
\sigma_{j}
maps
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
homeomorphically onto
\mathcal{C}_{j,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
for some
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbf{nt}_{j,j}
and so
0\neq[\sigma_{j}\circ\ell^{\prime}]\in\pi_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha^{\prime}})
. Since
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha^{\prime}}
is a retract of
Z_{j}
, we have
0\neq[\sigma_{j}\circ\ell^{\prime}]\in\pi_{n}(Z_{j})
.

\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{j}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}_{\leq j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{j}}
\textstyle{(I^{n},\partial I^{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ell}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\ell^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{j}}
\textstyle{Z_{j}}

Thus
0\neq[\sigma_{j}\circ\ell^{\prime}]=[\sigma_{j}\circ\widetilde{f}\circ\widetilde{\ell}]=[\widetilde{f}_{j}\circ\varrho_{j}\circ\widetilde{\ell}]
. Since
\widetilde{f}_{j}
is a homotopy equivalence,
0\neq[\varrho_{j}\circ\widetilde{\ell}]
in
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{Y}_{\leq j})
. Since
p_{\leq j}:\widetilde{Y}_{\leq j}\to Y_{\leq j}
induces an isomorphism on
\pi_{n}
, we have
0\neq[p_{\leq j}\circ\varrho_{j}\circ\widetilde{\ell}]=[r_{j}\circ p\circ\widetilde{\ell}]=[r_{j}\circ\ell]
in
\pi_{n}(Y_{j})
. We conclude that there exists
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
[r_{j}\circ\ell]\neq 0
. Therefore,
\Phi([\ell])\neq 0
.
∎

Remark 6.8
.

Recall from the introduction that standard homotopy theory gives
\pi_{n}(X_{\leq k})\cong\bigoplus_{1\leq j\leq k}\bigoplus_{\pi_{1}(X_{\leq k})/\pi_{1}(X_{j})}\pi_{n}(X_{j})
when each
\widetilde{X}_{j}
is
(n-1)
-connected. Under this hypothesis, the previous corollary provides a canonical injection of
\pi_{n}(X)
into

\varprojlim_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\bigoplus_{1\leq j\leq k}\bigoplus_{\pi_{1}(X_{\leq k})/\pi_{1}(X_{j})}\pi_{n}(X_{j})\right).

We point out that this inverse limit does not simply give the product over
k
because the bonding maps are not product-projections. Rather, the bonding maps correspond to the induced homomorphisms
(\widetilde{q}_{k+1,k})_{\#}:\pi_{n}(\widetilde{X}_{\leq k+1})\to\pi_{n}(\widetilde{X}_{\leq k})
.

6.4
The aspherical case

A path-connected space
X
is
aspherical
if
\pi_{n}(X)=0
for all
n\geq 2
. If
X
admits a generalized universal covering
\widetilde{X}
, then
\widetilde{X}
has trivial homotopy groups. If
X
is an aspherical CW-complex, then
\widetilde{X}
is contractible.

Lemma 6.9
.

If
Y_{j}
is aspherical for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
is a deformation retract of
Z
. In particular,
\mu:Z\to\mathbf{E}_{\infty}
is homotopic to
id_{Z}
.

Proof.

Suppose
Y_{j}
is aspherical for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Then each universal covering space
\widetilde{Y}_{j}
is contractible. For all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, we have

\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}=\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\infty,j,\alpha}/\mathcal{T}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\cong\widetilde{Y}_{j}/\beta_{\alpha}T_{j}\simeq\widetilde{Y}_{j},

Thus
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
is contractible. For each
\beta\in\pi_{1}(Y_{j})
, fix a based contraction
K_{j,\beta}:C_{j,\beta}\times I\to C_{j,\beta}
, i.e. a based homotopy from
id_{C_{j,\beta}}
to the constant map at
c_{j,\beta}
. Define contractions
L_{k}:Z_{k}\times I\to Z_{k}
so that
L_{k}(z,t)=z
for
(z,t)\in\mathbf{E}_{k}\times I
and so that the restriction of
L_{k}
to the subcomplex
\mathcal{C}_{k,j,\alpha}\times I
is a map
\mathcal{C}_{k,j,\alpha}\times I\to\mathcal{C}_{k,j,\alpha}
making the following square commute when
\Lambda_{k,j,\alpha}(\mathcal{T}_{k,j,\alpha})=\beta T_{j}
.

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{k,j,\alpha}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{k,j,\alpha}\times id}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{k,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{k,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{C_{j,\beta}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{K_{j,\beta}}
\textstyle{C_{j,\beta}}

The maps
L_{k}
are clearly well-defined and since
Z_{k}\times I
is a CW-complex,
L_{k}
is continuous. The same inductive argument used to construct the maps
\widetilde{g}_{k,j,\alpha}
can be used to show that
L_{k}\circ(\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}\times id)=\widetilde{s}_{k+1,k}\circ L_{k+1}
. Let
\widehat{L}_{0}:\widehat{Z}_{0}\times I\to\widehat{Z}_{0}
be the restriction of the inverse limit map
\widehat{L}=\varprojlim_{k}L_{k}:\widehat{Z}\times I\to\widehat{Z}
.

Define
L:Z\times I\to Z
as follows:
L(z,t)=z
for
(z,t)\in\mathbf{E}_{\infty}\times I
. For each
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbf{nt}_{\infty,j}
, we define the restriction of
L
to the subspace
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I
to be the based contraction
\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I\to\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}
which makes the following square commute.

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}\times id}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{\infty,j,\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\infty,j,\alpha}}
\textstyle{C_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}\times I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{K_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}
\textstyle{C_{j,\beta_{\alpha}}}

Clearly
L
is well-defined,
L(z,0)=z
and
L(z,1)=\mu(z)
. We have constructed
L
so that
\sigma_{k}\circ L=L_{k}\circ(\sigma_{k}\times id)
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
(details required to verify this are identical to previous arguments, e.g. the construction of
\widetilde{g}_{k}
). Since
Z\times I
is locally path connected and the projections
\sigma_{k}\circ L
are continuous,
L
is continuous by Corollary
5.24
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Suppose each
X_{j}
is aspherical. Then each
Y_{j}
is aspherical. We have the following sequence of maps.

\textstyle{\widetilde{X}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\zeta}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{\mathbf{E}_{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{g}|_{\mathbf{E}_{\infty}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{T}_{\infty}}

All of these maps except for
\mu
are always homotopy equivalences. Since each
Y_{j}
is aspherical, Lemma
6.9
implies that
\mu
is a homotopy equivalence. Since every uniquely arcwise connected Hausdorff space is aspherical,
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
is aspherical. Thus
\widetilde{X}
is aspherical. Moreover, if each
X_{j}
is locally finite, Corollary
5.35
implies that
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
is contractible. Hence, in this case, the sequence gives that
\widetilde{X}
is contractible.
∎
